#2950. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2947. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2945. Hyperscalers and Apple surge after election results remove investor uncertainty

Many of the biggest technology companies are surging on Wednesday, after Democrats scored a seismic victory in the midterm elections, flipping both the House and Senate.

During trading on Wednesday, Amazon surged 8%, Google rose over 6%, Microsoft rose 4%, Meta was up 3%, and Apple — which faced Trump's tariffs and recently got refunds — climbed nearly 7%.

Tuesday's results remove uncertainty in the market, David Kelly, chief global strategist at JPMorgan Asset Management, said on "Squawk on the Street."

He said the divided government would lead to "moderation in growth, moderation in inflation, less uncertainty, and I think that's all helping the stock market move up today."

However, some stocks, particularly those tied to Trump advisor Elon Musk as well as the AI data center buildout, fell on Wednesday.

As results came in last night, some observers saw bad news for big technology companies because of their bets to build large AI-focused data centers. There were 41 local ballot measures on Tuesday to place restrictions or clear the way for AI data centers, and voters sided against data centers in 40 of them. In a CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their voting decision.

Analysts said that Democrats who proposed data center moratoriums won gubernatorial races in Texas, Georgia, and Ohio, raising fears that projects could get snarled in regulation or blocked by politicians.

"Recall that backup power emissions, zoning, water, interconnect/grid, and tax considerations are often overseen by state regulators," Macquarie analyst Paul Golding said in a note on Wednesday.

Tesla stock fell 2%, and SpaceX was down 3%. Nvidia, which makes the chips for most of these AI data center projects, slipped just over 1%.

Many of the technology stocks rising on Wednesday are what are known as hyperscalers, or the largest cloud providers that also pair massive capex spending with robust free cash flow and have the most experience and capability to construct AI data centers.

"We believe higher regulatory barriers entrench incumbents, and increase risks (and raise required ROICs) for new entrants," wrote Needham analyst Laura Martin in a note on Wednesday. "Hyperscalers are AI incumbents."

Smaller AI cloud providers, often called neoclouds, got whacked on Wednesday. CoreWeave stock fell 10%. Baird analysts said in a note that the election results create a "sentiment headwind" for neoclouds.

Golding, the Macquarie analyst, wrote that he remains bullish on AI infrastructure, and that well-capitalized, high-quality operators stand to benefit the most if projects in some states slow down.

"Neoclouds may pay more to lease powered shells for GPUaaS commitments (and pass on costs), while hyperscaler/AI lab capacity needs could intensify competition for even more limited supply," Golding wrote.

Tuesday's results could signal restrictions on new data center developments in some states, but it doesn't necessarily signal a nationwide slowdown in construction, Chris Miglino, CEO of Axe Compute, said in an email. Companies are still raising capital, enterprises are spending, and there's still a need for more capacity.

Miglino said that increased regulation in some regions could cause investors and customers to look more closely at different cloud computing providers, and opt for those with the capability to deliver capacity, or which offer AI inference to a specific industry niche.

"If you've already secured power, have the financing in place and understand how to deploy and operate compute infrastructure efficiently, you're in a much stronger position than a company that's still trying to figure those things out," Miglino said.

#2940. Snap shares fall over 10% on third-quarter revenue miss

Snap shares sank over 10% when the Snapchat parent missed on revenue and daily active users in its third-quarter earnings report Wednesday.

Here's how the company did compared with analysts' expectations:

Sales in Snap's third quarter jumped 14% year-over-year while net income came in at $56 million, compared to the $104 million net loss it logged the prior year.

Snap's third-quarter adjusted earnings came in at $383 million, topping the $337 million that analysts were projecting, according to StreetAccount.

The Snapchat parent said fourth-quarter revenue should come in between $1.95 billion and $1.99 billion. The midpoint of that figure is higher than Wall Street's estimates of $1.95 billion.

Fourth-quarter adjusted earnings are expected to come in between $490 million and $525 million. Analysts were expecting that figure to be $498 million.

Snap executives said in an investor letter that the company plans for globally monthly active users to become its "primary audience metric" starting in its first quarter for 2027. The company also said it has 977 MAU for the quarter.

"Daily engagement has historically been stable as a percentage of monthly engagement, but we do not intend to optimize Snapchat around daily use at the expense of long-term value for our community," the investor letter said. "A longer-term horizon better aligns our reported metrics with the product and safety decisions that strengthen relationships, create lasting utility, and support broader, more durable audience growth."

Snap chief commercial officer Ronan Harris told analysts during an earnings call that lower spending by unspecified consumer packaged goods, or CPG, companies impacted third-quarter sales. Harris attributed the curbed CPG spending to "macro pressures in the U.S. and also the Middle East.""As we look ahead, we're seeing very positive signs of growth from diversified verticals like financial services in the U.S.," Harris said. "So part of our strategy here is to look outside of the pressures in CPG and find traction in these other verticals."

#2934. Figma sinks after earnings beat as company prioritizes long-term growth with AI changes

Figma shares tumbled 7% in extended trading after the company reported third-quarter results and guidance that surpassed Wall Street's predictions.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

Figma's revenue grew about 43% year over year in the quarter, according to a statement. Net loss narrowed to $81.3 million, or 15 cents per share, compared with a loss of $1.10 billion, or $2.72 per share, a year earlier.

The company's 12% adjusted operating margin exceeded the 6.1% consensus among analysts polled by StreetAccount.

Executives lifted the full-year forecast. Figma now anticipates an 11% operating margin, up from 9% as of August, and $1.497 billion to $1.499 billion in revenue, compared with $1.463 billion to $1.467 billion before. Analysts surveyed by LSEG were looking for $1.471 billion in 2026 revenue.

The company sees fourth-quarter revenue growth slowing to about 33%. Analysts polled by LSEG had predicted 28% growth.

Figma's announcement comes a day after highly valued design software startup Canva said it would cut 22% of its employees.

Shares have come down about 41% so far in 2026, while the S&P 500 index has gained 12%. Earlier this year, investors became concerned that artificial intelligence models could pose a competitive risk to Figma. Across much of software, AI panic has largely subsided as incumbents have posted persistent growth.

Figma wants to benefit from the rising adoption of AI to build websites and apps. During the quarter, the company started giving more AI credits to clients while maintaining prices.

"What that means is you're potentially trading off near-term revenue, investing in your customers, and then allowing yourself to then grow durably over the long term," Figma's finance chief, Praveer Melwani, told CNBC in an interview on Wednesday.

The number of weekly users working at organizations that spend over $10,000 on Figma who used AI credits increased by almost 50% during the quarter, Figma said in the statement. The share of work the company's own AI model performs doubled, and the rate of people undoing AI-generated work has fallen.

"If there's certain actions that we can power for our customers at lower latency, lower cost, or similar quality to third party, we're open to that as well," Melwani said.

Figma is increasing its focus on cybersecurity, said Dylan Field, Figma's co-founder and CEO. In recent months, AI agents have infiltrated systems at Hugging Face and other companies.

Clients aren't asking Figma to become a security vendor, but the company must scrutinize "the security of all of our vendors right now," Field said.

#2928. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2924. Qualcomm stock drops 6% on weak earnings forecast as memory shortage weighs on smartphone sales

Qualcomm reported quarterly results on Wednesday that topped Wall Street estimates, but the chipmaker's earnings forecast came in lighter than expected. The stock slid 6% in extended trading.

Here's how the chipmaker did versus LSEG consensus estimates:

For the current quarter, Qualcomm said it sees earnings per share of between $2.15 and $2.35 on revenue of $10.4 billion to $11.2 billion. Analysts expected earnings per share of $2.27 on revenue of $10.48 billion.

Most of Qualcomm's chip sales come from system-on-chips and modems for smartphones. It's a market that's currently in turmoil as rising memory costs force handset makers to raise prices, hurting demand. Adjusted company revenue fell 7% during from a year earlier.

Qualcomm raised prices in September. Still, handset chip sales during the quarter fell 26% to $5.14 billion, trailing a StreetAccount estimate of $5.21 billion. Qualcomm said it's starting to see sequential growth in the category, and that Android shipments in China have bottomed, even though the company predicts the handset market will be flat in 2027.

Under CEO Cristiano Amon, Qualcomm has tried to turn investor attention to other kinds of chips. The company is looking to enter the fast-growing market for AI data center infrastructure. It had $650 million in data center revenue during the quarter, mostly from customizing and connectivity work, Amon said.

Amon said Qualcomm expects $5 billion in data center revenue in fiscal 2027. He said the company recently signed a customer for optical connectivity and had engaged a global hyperscaler.

"We don't have a demand issue; it's a supply issue," Amon said. "If I had more supply, I would probably be doing better."

Qualcomm also sells chips for cars and other automotive uses. Revenue in that division soared 89% on an annual basis to $1.99 billion, beating StreetAccount expectations of $1.66 billion.

Internet of things revenue includes low-power chips, smart glasses SoCs, laptop chips, as well as the company's bid to provide cellular infrastructure. Earlier on Wednesday, Qualcomm announced a deal to provide technology to SpaceX's cellular buidlout. IoT revenue increased 4% from a year earlier to $1.88 billion, slightly higher than estimates.

Qualcomm's QTL division includes revenue from licensing its intellectual property for 5G and other technologies to other companies. Qualcomm said during the quarter that it had renewed licensing agreements with Apple, Huawei, and Transsion.

"QTL will maintain its scale going forward," Amon said.

QTL revenue sank 7% to $1.3 billion, in line with a StreetAccount estimate.

#2922. Prediction markets cement their status after forecasting Democratic sweep of Congress

Prediction market platforms cemented their role in election forecasting on Tuesday, when markets accurately predicted a host of races before the polls closed, and projected outcomes before news outlets.

Democrats were seen as favored to flip both the U.S. House of Representatives and Senate on prediction markets before the first polls closed at 6:00 p.m. ET Tuesday, and were seen as narrow favorites to take at least 53 Senate seats, while a 54th was forecast as a toss-up.

So far, Democrats have won 54 Senate seats — including wins in Iowa, which prediction markets placed at 50-50 odds, and South Carolina, where Republicans narrowly favored. Outstanding results remain in Mississippi and Alaska, where prediction markets give Democrat Scott Colom and Mary Peltola 90% odds of winning.

Leading prediction market platforms were actively projecting races on X Tuesday night. Those projections, at times, came before many media outlets made their own projections, or before even a substantial number of votes had been counted.

Kalshi was confident making those calls, as long as odds for a candidate were above 99% and trading activity for the opposite candidate "dried out,"  said Jack Such, a spokesperson for the platform.

Polymarket celebrated on X how it projected races, saying that in 10 key races, the platform called races ahead of outlets including CNN, Fox News and the Associated Press by a median 31 minutes.

"It's now routinely hours between when prediction markets call it versus the TV networks," said Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan in an X post. "No knock to them, but now there's Polymarket, and many people are realizing they want to know what's happening in real time."

Even in races where markets initially favored the candidate who eventually lost, they quickly adjusted once initial vote counts came in. In South Carolina, Democratic Senate candidate Annie Andrews was seen as the favorite on Kalshi to win shortly after 7:30 p.m., only 30 minutes after polls closed. In Texas, where Democratic state Rep. Gina Hinojosa ousted Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, Hinojosa's odds on Polymarket jumped from 39% to 73% within five minutes of initial poll closings.

"You now have financially motivated number crunchers doing this, around the world, that give you that signal just that much faster," said Flip Pidot, a member of the board of directors at the Coalition for Political Forecasting.

Still, a successful night for prediction markets shouldn't stop efforts aimed at stronger regulation of the platforms, said Michelle Kendler-Kretsch, a researcher at the Anti-Corruption Data Collective, a nonprofit. She cited a recent Reuters/Ipsos survey that found most Americans are against using prediction markets to trade on elections.

"Risks and vulnerabilities didn't change overnight just because the markets were accurate this time around," she said.

Right before the midterms, prediction market forecasts had missed major global elections, including Brazil's presidential race, where Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva only had about 20% odds to win on Kalshi before polls closed.

Prediction markets also whiffed in some high-profile Democratic primaries over the summer, forecasting extremely high odds for progressives in Wisconsin and Michigan, for example. The Michigan Democrat, Abdul El-Sayed, narrowly won his primary before going on to win Thursday's general election.

Academics who've researched prediction markets for decades say the platforms aren't wrong when candidates with low odds on markets end up winning races.

"If you toss two coins, there's a 25% chance that you'll throw two heads in a row, and nobody would be terribly surprised if you did," said Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College.

Despite their early success on Tuesday, Dartmouth College economic professor Eric Zitzewitz said it's still not clear if casual observers understand how market probabilities work.

"Some people who are coming to [prediction markets] for the first time will see 55 by 45 odds and think that, 'Oh the candidate with 55 is way ahead,'" he said.

As prediction markets gain traction, however, the public will improve at understanding how to interpret their odds, Zitzewitz said.

With another election under their belts, prediction markets will remain a force through the next presidential election, experts said.

"Last time around [in 2024], prediction markets were in the discussion, but they were in the discussion in a way that was a little bit unsure," said Harry Crane, a professor of statistics at Rutgers University. "This [was] the first true prediction markets election."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2920. The Celtics are showing progress attacking the rim, but there’s room to grow

In his season-ending press conference immediately after Boston's first-round playoff exit, Brad Stevens made a comment about the state of the Celtics that reverberated through every offseason decision leading up to the start of the season.

"I think one of the things that we've gotta figure out is how to have more impact at the rim," Stevens said. "I thought we struggled to get to where we wanted to go on offense."

He had a point. In the regular season, the Celtics were dead last in volume inside of 5 feet, averaging 22 attempts per game. But on low volume came high efficiency, with the team's 69% finishing on those attempts being second in the league. On top of that lack of attempts was a league-low in free throw attempts (18.7).

So, the Celtics made changes. They brought in Mitchell Robinson, one of the game's best offensive rebounders after the Celtics averaged the lowest amount of putback points among all playoff teams (4.1). Then they swapped Paul George for Jaylen Brown, adding a shot creator that would offer a different kind of off-ball influence to open up Jayson Tatum's own scoring creation.

As of Nov. 2, Boston's 7-game starting sample has shown early returns in this area. As far as just touching the paint, they're getting inside at an increased rate, jumping from 24.4 touches to 28.3. The Celtics entered their Rockets matchup 13th in field goal attempts inside of 5 feet, making a major leap of 28.3 attempts to last year's 22.

There's work to be done in their finishing on those attempts — they're currently working at a 63% clip (17th in the league) — but the results are showing a team that's making an effort to get more looks at close range.

This back-to-back showed two sides of that progress, with slight variations in personnel and significant variations in Joe Mazzulla's lineups.

On the first night of their back-to-back, the Celtics got an Orlando team that's missing key pieces and is in the process of developing a new identity under coach Sean Sweeney, yet still retains some of that gritty defensive identity with their available personnel.

For Boston, only Mitchell Robinson was absent from the rotation, and in that absence, we saw more of the freeflowing, fast-reacting movement of last year's offense with Neemias Queta as the lone big on the floor.

It's no surprise that Robinson and Queta lead the team in paint touches, but behind them stand Boston's two most important players in Tatum (3.5) and George (3). In this matchup, they shined both in their self-creation and in their playmaking inside the paint.

As a team Boston was 72% inside the restricted area, and Tatum's gravity as a pick-and-roll ballhandler was a significant factor in creating looks for himself and for the roller.

Tatum's high free throw rate has also been a major benefit of his driving aggression. The Celtics are currently averaging 28 attempts from the line, another significant jump from last year's number. Tatum is currently at 10.2 attempts from the line.

Like Tatum, George is creating off the dribble, feeding the roll man, and comfortably playing off-ball.

It's two players who have figured out how to put the ball into space. But for that to happen, you need good cutting instincts from those around them. That wasn't an issue last year, and it's definitely not an issue now. The Celtics are currently scoring 1.35 points per cut possession, nearly identical to last year's 1.36 PPP.

This game was a good indicator of this team's ceiling attacking the rim. You're missing Robinson, who has struggled to adapt to his new role closer to the action on the perimeter, but you're seeing Boston's other two bigs look comfortable in their roles, while the team's two stars acted as high-running motors to an efficient offense. When you can space the floor with shooters around Tatum and George and add in a rolling threat like Queta, the space to attack and the decisions you can make feel more abundant.

On the second night of their back-to-back, the Celtics were without their other primary offseason addition with Paul George getting a night off.

On a sluggish shooting night, the primary theme for this story's subject was as follows: No Tatum=No Paint Pressure.

Without George's primary option lineup, when Tatum stepped off the floor, getting inside the arc became a chore for Boston. In a shade under 16 minutes with Tatum resting, they were just 1-for-8 on shots inside the paint, with a single shot generated at the rim. With Tatum, they had 20 shots at the rim and were 70% on those shots, much more in line with what we've seen so far this season.

Along with his ability to create in isolation, Tatum's gravity as a pick-and-roll threat opened up rim-runs for Mitchell Robinson and Queta, while his vision finding cutters particularly flashed early on.

The Celtics trailed by five when Tatum checked out with 1:39 left in the third quarter. When he returned with 7:02 left to go in the fourth quarter, it was a deficit of 16. With JT off the floor in general, the Celtics were outscored 35-18.

When he returned, it didn't take long for the offense to get back into its previous flow. Tatum quickly assisted on a drive-and-kick three to Derrick White in the corner, hit Queta on a roll, and scored himself on a drive-and-finish within his first six possessions after checking back in. But by then, the Rockets had comfortably separated enough to bury them.

With a lack of paint pressure coming from the perimeter, Mazzulla experimented heavily with double-big rotations throughout the game, which in spurts gave Boston some more putback and lob options but wasn't nearly enough to overcome poor shooting from the outside and a tough night on drives. The Queta-Robinson combo was featured most prominently, finishing the night with 4:42 on the floor together. With those two sharing the floor, the Celtics were 3-for-3 at the rim, two of which came from Queta and the other from Robinson.

Even without an interior enforcer like Steven Adams, the Rockets are an exceptionally difficult team to get a step on. They have a collection of lengthy wing defenders like Amen Thompson, Tari Eason and Kevin Durant and feisty point-of-attack guards in Marcus Smart, Fred VanVleet and Reed Sheppard. Add to that disciplined help on the nail, and even if your last line of defense is Alperen Sengun, the defense is designed to make it drudgerous just to get to that weak link. And it just so happens that their weak link still finished with a pair of blocks and steals on Monday night.

If the Orlando game was a sign of what they could be, Houston was a sign of how far they have to go to get there. Missing George's ballhandling was a predictable blow, but a lack of influence in the paint from those not named Jayson Tatum against a quality defense does show that they have a lot to work on.

The road ahead is long to figure out what kind of impact this team can have from close range. They can't all be against a Chicago, and they won't all be against a Houston or Detroit, but it's important that every step of the way, we keep tabs on the progress of this team's still-developing interior identity.

#2918. What Republicans' huge loss means for Trump

Democrats just put President Donald Trump in a bind.

The party is projected to win commanding majorities in the House and Senate after thrashing Republicans in key races up and down the ballot across the country. The blue tsunami will knock Washington on its head and crush the Republican congressional bulwark that both supported and shielded Trump through the first two years of his term.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The midterm result snuffs out any chance Trump had of passing partisan legislation in the second half of his presidency. And many of the interests he has been able to pursue without Congress' input — including tariffs, the Iran war and an array of Washington-area construction projects — are sure to face sudden pushback from newly empowered Democratic majorities.

The paradigm shift hardly leaves Trump helpless, however. As president at a time of executive power expansion, Trump wields singular authorities that could stymie Democrats' efforts to pursue their own agendas.

But with his grip on the Capitol shriveling and his approval ratings scraping record lows in numerous polls, it's unclear how much support he'll be able to muster to fight the coming political battles.

Even before Election Day, analysts and pundits had contemplated a Democratic rout so decisive that it would force a reckoning for the GOP, and potentially even pose an existential threat to Trump's "Make America Great Again" movement.

Marc Short, chairman of Advancing American Freedom and onetime chief of staff to former Vice President Mike Pence, told CNBC on Tuesday afternoon that the GOP has moved away from its conservative foundations.

"We need a refresh right now," Short said in an interview. "We've lost our way."

Trump has been vague about what he hopes to achieve with the rest of his term. But he has shared a vision of how he might coexist with a Democratic majority, and it involves heavy use of the veto pen.

"It's one of the stronger powers that a president has," Trump said in a Fox News interview overnight. "They'll send over things that are insane, and I'll veto them."

"And after a couple of months of vetoes, they'll call and they'll say, 'How do we get this stuff done?' And we'll work together," he said, adding, "I have a lot of friends that are Democrats."

Trump has previously stated his willingness to veto reams of legislation that Democrats send across his desk. In a New York Post podcast interview released Monday, Trump said that if Republicans don't win, "I veto everything."

Pressed to detail his plans for the next two years, Trump touted his record and said, "The most important thing is to keep it going. It's going to get better and better and better, unless people come in that hate our country."

If that happens, "I guess I'll become Mr. Veto," Trump said.

He may soon get his chance: House Democrats, poised to be led by current Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York, are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned.

The legislative push, first reported by The New York Times, aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to introduce bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law earlier this year.

Democrats also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the war with Iran, or seek congressional authorization to keep it going.

But without Trump's approval, those bills may end up as little more than symbolic gestures, as Democrats will lack the two-thirds majorities in both chambers that are required to override a presidential veto without GOP help.

And there's no guarantee the bills will even make it out of Congress: Despite their landslide victories, Senate Democrats will still end up shy of the 60 votes required to bypass the chamber's filibuster rules for most legislation, giving Republicans key leverage in the minority.

Democrats also could feasibly benefit from Trump's vetoes in the long term. Forcing the president to shoot down numerous popular bills could give them fodder in the 2028 election.

It's far from clear whether Trump believes he can find compromise with Democrats on any of their initial legislative priorities.

But any potential for a working relationship with the opposition could be poisoned early on, as Democrats in both chambers are aggressively signaling their plans to launch politically charged investigations against Trump, his political and corporate allies and even his family members.

Winning control of House committees gives Democrats the ability to issue subpoenas under the current rules.

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., who is likely to chair the House Oversight Committee in the next Congress, said his panel will target the Trump family's business dealings, corporate corruption and alleged abuses by federal immigration agencies. He also plans to return focus to the Trump administration's handling of files related to notorious sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, Garcia summed up his plans in a two-word statement: "Lawyer up."

It's not just Garcia. Other Democrats have already demanded records from the Trump administration on a range of issues. Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., for instance, last week accused Trump of using tariff exemptions to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

Senate Democrats have sent similar signals.

"They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we'll be printing," Sen. Ed Markey, D-Mass., said in an X post late Tuesday in response to a post, appearing to refer to tech executives.

The investigations are widely expected to consume much of the rest of Trump's term.

Trump has ample experience being targeted by Democrats, who impeached him twice in the House during his first presidential term.

But with his base of support appearing to erode inside and outside of Washington, Trump may have to face his political foes with a smaller arsenal than he's used to.

"I'm not expecting Republicans to revolt" against Trump following the midterm losses, AAF's Short told CNBC. "But I'm expecting some of them to quietly distance themselves."

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2915. NBA power rankings: Rockets and Sixers shoot to top, Knicks drop

The NBA paused its action to encourage Americans to vote.

So with no games scheduled for Tuesday, Nov. 3, it offered a bit of a reset to take in the lessons learned from the first two weeks of the 2026-27 regular season.

The good news for teams is that the spate of injuries that ravaged rosters across the league have (mostly) calmed. And while it's still so very early in the season, the early action has led to sweeping changes across the board. Already, there's a new No. 1 team, and a new No. 2 for that matter.

Here are the Week 2 USA TODAY Sports NBA power rankings:

They're second-to-last on net rating (-13.2) and are prime candidates to sell off their assets before the trading deadline.

No team plays faster than the Bulls, who lead in pace (105.86), but the offense has been wildly inefficient, with Chicago shooting just 41.6%, second-worst in the NBA.

The addition of Julius Randle hasn't been enough to stabilize the offense, and 3-point defense (15.0 conversions allowed per game) has been another Achilles heel.

The Jazz, who were looking to take a step forward, have been one of the more disappointing teams. Utah doesn't force enough turnovers and its transition offense is basically nonexistent.

Tyler Herro has been pretty solid, but too many Bucks players have been far too inconsistent, starting with Kel'el Ware.

The Clippers still are the only team to average at least 20 turnovers per game.

They're the only NBA team to have only played five games, but Phoenix has been getting killed on the glass, where they rank 29th in rebounding (39.6 per game).

Indiana is banged up, but without Tyrese Haliburton, this offense lacks a catalyst and identity.

Winners of three consecutive, the Pelicans have been a nice story so far and are dominating down low; New Orleans leads the league with 62.6 points in the paint per game … which is 7.5 points more than the next team.

Since Paolo Banchero (ankle) and Jalen Suggs (knee) have been out, Orlando has been unable to find consistent offense and turnovers (17.3 per game; tied for 25th) are a massive issue.

No team is allowing more 3-pointers per game (17.3). The defense has been a nightmare, with players even saying they're not trusting each other on the court.

They've lost four consecutive and the warning sign is an offense that has is drastically underperforming; Charlotte ranks dead last in offensive rating (100.4), which is nearly four full points below the next closest team.

Are the Wizards finally a player in the East? The early returns on this group look good, but the schedule hasn't exactly been a slog.

They look older and slower, but the more curious issue is the sweeping dip across Nikola Jokić's stats: 22.0 points, 9.7 rebounds and 9.0 assists per game.

They've won three of four, but became the first team to lose to the previously winless Kings. The Cooper Flagg-Kyrie Irving partnership, however, has been a fun watch.

They've had five different leading scorers through eight games and lead the NBA in assists per game (30.6) and assist-to-turnover ratio (2.08). The results have nonetheless been uneven.

Are we in championship hangover territory? The Knicks are a bit of a rarity, ranking dead last in pace (96.5). They've struggled against marquee opponents.

No team has been hauling in more offensive boards per game (15.3), but losses in three of their last five have stunted their progress up the standings. And they got good news: Kawhi Leonard (knee swelling) might be back soon.

They share the ball well and protect it, but the Cavs have had an uneven start to the season. A loss against an extremely short-handed Thunder team was a missed opportunity.

They've won five consecutive, some against top-level teams (Lakers, Timberwolves, Nuggets). And Stephen Curry, at 38 years old, is playing his way into the MVP conversation.

Minnesota's two victories this week were against the Clippers and the Bucks, which is fine. But the Timberwolves will need to show that they can beat some of the league's better teams without Anthony Edwards. The saving grace will be the defense.

The overtime loss against the Pelicans was a setback, but Memphis remains the biggest surprise in the NBA, and Ty Jerome (28.2 points per game; seventh in the NBA) has been a revelation.

Losing Ja Morant (hamstring) is certainly a blow, but Portland has been doing a great job on the glass, limiting teams to an 10.0 second chance points per game.

The move to add Mitchell Robinson is paying off so far, as pairing him alongside Neemias Queta has Boston ranking third in the league in opponent points in the paint per game (39.4).

Recent competition hasn't been as robust, but the Spurs have won five consecutive and are taking care of the ball, ranking second in turnovers per game (13.4).

Luka Dončić is flaming opponents, averaging an absurd 38.3 points per game and orchestrating the NBA's best offense (121.9 points per 100 possessions).

They've dropped two of their last three, as the injuries keep piling up. The latest, to Chet Holmgren, means OKC will be without three starters for the foreseeable future.

It's rather unfair to drop a team one spot even though it won all three games this week. This is more about how good the 76ers have been and the quality of opponents Detroit faced. But can the Pistons sustain this level with Duncan Robinson (a career-high 17.9 points per game) as their second-leading scorer?

Interestingly, it's the defense that has been Philly's strength. The Sixers have the league's third-best defensive rating (105.1 points allowed per 100 possessions).

They own the NBA's top net rating (+12.8), Alperen Şengün is anchoring the offense and Jabari Smith Jr. is breaking out.

#2912. Analysis: The Democrats have one chance to extract concessions from Trump. Markets will hate it

The electoral sweep by Democrats on Tuesday night injects a new, unstable dynamic into the single biggest political risk to financial markets in the coming year: the debt limit. Unless a defeated Republican Party makes a swift and unlikely show of unity to take the issue off the table in its last few weeks in power, the protracted fight that will unfold is set to be nastier than ever, adding volatility to an already shaky bond market.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

Past episodes of brinkmanship around the need to raise the government's borrowing limit have involved Republicans in Congress demanding budget cuts from a Democratic president as the price of their vote. This time it will almost certainly be the left seeking concessions from the right.

The debt limit is the definition of a must-pass bill, even more than issues like funding the government and the defense budget. That makes it potentially the one real chance the new Democratic majority in Congress has to extract concessions from a president who is already pledging to veto their agenda. Markets will hate the entire process, even if — as many expect — it ends in compromise.

Because it is Democrats who hold the leverage, unlike past episodes, the ultimate agreement is more likely to increase government spending than lower it. With high bond yields weighing on the economy, and Americans expecting action from a newly empowered Democratic opposition, the fight could be particularly damaging.

Many in the markets are stuck on the outdated idea that Democrats are less assertive than Republicans, said Aniket Shah, head of the Washington policy team at the investment bank Jefferies.

"They have this sort of mental image of Biden-Harris 2024," said Shah. "I don't think they realize how aggressively Democrats plan to take 2027."

The debt ceiling is a quirk of the congressional budgeting process. It stops the government from borrowing funds that Congress has already ordered federal agencies to spend. And because Congress has run deficits for decades — this year at $1.99 trillion — the pile of debt periodically bumps into the debt ceiling.

Congress last set the limit in 2025, when it gave the Treasury Department the authority to borrow $41.1 trillion. The Treasury is likely to hit its maximum borrowing capacity by midsummer, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonprofit that tracks the issue.

The Treasury can use budgeting techniques to extend the so-called X-date, when the U.S. would no longer be able to meet all its obligations, to sometime in the fourth quarter of 2027, according to Wall Street estimates.

A few technical hiccups aside, the U.S. has never meaningfully breached the debt limit. The results of the U.S. missing payments would be chaotic as damage ripples out across the financial system into the global economy. U.S. default would immediately call into question trillions in Treasury securities that are the lifeblood of the global financial system. Damaging them could send the U.S. and global economies into an economic tailspin with no obvious means of recovery.

Because the stakes are so high, Shah and many others on Wall Street have come to believe that the episode will produce volatility but will ultimately be resolved in compromise.

Fights under former Presidents Barack Obama and Joe Biden saw Republican congressional majorities use the issue to ram through spending reductions. (The 2025 raise was less contentious, because Republicans held Congress and the presidency. It was passed along party lines.)

Still, the risk-taking in those episodes contributed to ratings firms' decisions to downgrade U.S. debt — and could again.

President Donald Trump has called for Republicans to resolve the issue in the next few weeks, before the new Democratic-majority Congress is seated.

That isn't likely. The 2025 debt limit increase only passed because it was tied to a package of tax cuts and other policy changes. Republican debt hawks complained but held their noses.

Republicans may attempt to raise the limit in the coming lame-duck session under a budgeting procedure called reconciliation, which would allow them to move ahead without Democrats' votes. But success would require unity among a fractured, fleeting Republican majority that also faces several other more pressing priorities.

The most likely path toward raising the debt limit is the incoming Democratic majority negotiating another temporary fix with Trump. But they aren't going to make it easy. The president has occasionally made noises about making deals, but the political incentives ahead of the 2028 presidential election are for both sides to dig in.

"[Democrats] are going to want to embarrass the White House, and they're going to want to get leverage," Shah said. Democrats will use that to push to reverse cuts to Medicaid spending passed in 2025 and to try to restore healthcare spending, he said.

Democrats could also seek to tie the debt limit to restraining signature Trump policies, such as limiting the war with Iran or rolling back tariffs, analysts at Goldman Sachs said in a research note Wednesday.

Another potential compromise being considered in some corners of Washington is for Democrats to tie an increase in the corporate tax rate to a debt limit raise, said George Pollack, senior U.S. analyst with Signum Global Advisors. Trump has shown he can be surprisingly flexible on similar issues, Pollack noted.

That is what will make this debt limit fight different. In the past, Republicans used it to try to win spending reductions they couldn't get through regular budget negotiations. Now Democrats will broaden it out into an all-purpose hostage. That effort is far more likely to raise overall government spending than cut it. "There's a very weird irony here," said Robert Sockin, chief U.S. economist at PGIM, an asset manager.

The path to compromise will likely be rocky. Sockin believes it may turn out like the 2025 government shutdown. That was the longest government-wide shutdown in history, even though Democrats weren't in control of either chamber of Congress. This time they will have even more power.

If the fight extends beyond the X-date, missing debt payments could do serious damage to the financial system.

"It's not our base case, but we think the risk is higher than ever that you could get a technical default from the government," Sockin said. That would see the U.S. miss a payment, even if the government eventually makes it up.

That would still hurt. "You'll get more downgrades to the U.S. sovereign, and those might bite more this time," Sockin said. Yields on longer-term Treasurys would rise to account for added uncertainty.

Crossing the red line could prompt a "very negative reaction in equity markets," he said.

The coming months will show just how close Democrats are willing to get to that line.

#2910. Patrick Dumont purchases Miriam Adelson’s share of Mavericks, now owns 69% of team

In 2023, Mark Cuban sold what was then his majority stake in the Dallas Mavericks to Miriam Adelson and her son-in-law, Patrick Dumont. At the time, the sale was purported to be to the tune of $3.5 billion. The transaction gave Adelson and Dumont majority ownership of the franchise, with Dumont serving in the role of Governor while Cuban maintained a minority stake in the team.

It is now being reported that another sale took place last month, though this one does not involve any new parties entering the fold. Adelson has instead sold her stake in the Mavericks to Dumont and his wife (and Adelson's daughter) Sivan Ochshorn. As a result of the deal, Dumont now holds a 69% ownership stake.

Along with Dumont are minority owners Cuban (27%) and Mary Stanton (4%). As part of the terms of the original sale in 2023, Dumont retains the right to purchase as much as 20% of Cuban's remaining portion as well. "Our plan is to look at that and to do that over time," Dumont suggests. Should he chose to do so, he would then hold a whopping 89% of the franchise which Forbes values at $5.8 billion.

Since taking over in 2023, Dumont has overhauled much of the front office. He hired Rick Welts as CEO, Ethan Casson as President (and successor CEO come the turn of the calendar to 2027), Masai Ujiri, Mike Schmitz, new head coach Dusty May and a number of other senior-manager level employees. Dumont is also hard at work on a new basketball-only area for the Mavs, with plans to make the former Valley View Mall an all-out entertainment destination.

"The most important thing every day is just trying to work harder to get better." Dumont clearly has big plans on where he wants to take the Mavericks behind young phenom Cooper Flagg and this purchase demonstrates his long-term commitment to the team

I invite you to follow me @_80MPH on X, and check back often at Mavs Moneyball for all the latest on the Dallas Mavericks.

#2909. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2905. Every stock that moved because of the election

The blue wave across the country has rippled through the stock market too, driving healthcare and clean energy names higher and data center plays and defense companies tumbling.

The broader market soared after Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate, and key gubernatorial races in Texas and Georgia. In Washington, the Democratic Congress will now serve as a counterweight to President Donald Trump's agenda, which has included tax cuts and policies that sent inflation, like tariffs and the Iran war.

Local and state elections will also have an impact on data center stocks, with construction limits passed in some states and a Democratic governor-elect in Texas who promised a moratorium.

"Democratic control could slow the push for deregulation in financials, energy and digital assets," Wells Fargo Investment Institute said in a note Wednesday. "We still believe both parties have an incentive to compromise on data center location and AI generally."

Here are some of the biggest movers as a result of the election outcome.

Nextpower jumped more than 11.2 % and Canadian Solar rallied 6.7%, while Plug Power gained 3.6%, First Solar climbed 4.6% and Sunrun ticked 1.3% higher. Nextera Energy added 4.9%. Investors are hoping that Democrats will push back against the Trump administration's rejection of clean energy policies.

Healthcare companies rallied on investor optimism over a slowdown or reversal in healthcare spending cuts, and the potential that enhanced subsidies will extended in Affordable Care Act plans.

Molina Healthcare and Centene gained 8.9% and 12.6%, respectively. HCA Healthcare soared 10.3% and Universal Health Services jumped 10.9%, while Tenet Healthcare rallied 9.6% and Elevance Health added 4.9%.

Elon Musk's SpaceX and Tesla shed 3.8% and 3.3%, respectively, after Democrats won the gubernatorial, Senate, and attorney general races in Texas, as well as wresting control of the Texas House of Representatives . Musk, CEO of both companies, spent at least $474 million backing Republican candidates in the midterms, including $224 million in the Lone Star State, where both Tesla and SpaceX are headquartered.

Defense companies could face headwinds if Democrats challenge military spending. Shares of RTX slipped 2.4%, Lockheed Martin tumbled 6.9%, Northrop Grumman sank 4.5% and Howmet Aerospace fell 4.3%. Palantir Technologies shed 2.1%.

The Trump administration has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027. It is also separately asking for an $87.6 billion supplemental appropriation from Congress for fiscal 2026, largely due to the Iran conflict.

Drone stocks also fell. Kratos Defense & Security Solutions dropped 12.3%, while AeroVironment lost 8.3% and Red Cat fell 10.4%. Ondas shed 7% and Unusual Machines tumbled 9%.

Names connected with data centers moved lower amid concern about the potential for restrictions or moratoriums being placed on AI infrastructure buildouts.

Neocloud provider CoreWeave, the poster child for fears over massive AI-related debt, slumped 9.9%. Peers Nebius and Iren dropped 6.7% and 11.2%, respectively.

AI infrastructure stocks also tumbled, with Comfort Systems losing 15.1% and Vistra sliding 13.8%. Micron Technology fell 7%.

That said, five of the Magnificent Seven rallied. Apple rose 5.3% and Microsoft jumped 6.7%, with Apple touching an all-time high. Alphabet gained 3.2% and Meta added 1.4%.

A consumer relief trade also took hold, sending housing, travel and consumer staples stocks higher. Affordability was a big theme in the election, with Americans feeling the pressure of high prices.

Housing stocks like Pulte, D.R. Horton and Lennar gained roughly 7.2%, 8.1% and 7.1%, respectively. Airlines also moved higher, with United Airlines rising 8% and American Airlines climbing 6.3%. Southwest Airlines jumped 6.8% and Delta Air Lines climbed 7.2%. Cruise operator Carnival increased 7.3%, while Royal Caribbean and Norwegian Cruise Line added 6.4% and 6.8%, respectively. Booking Holdings rallied 9.8%.

Consumer staple names also caught a bid. Dollar Tree soared 9.6%, Walmart gained 3.5% and Constellation Brands climbed 5.3%. Dollar General advanced 8.5%, while Conagra Brands increased 7%.

The GOP's wide losses also extended dragged down President Donald Trump's social media and tech company, which plunged 24.5% and hit a 52-week low.

— CNBC's Luke Fountain, Dominic Chu and Nicholas Wells contributed reporting.

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

#2901. YouTube Gold: The Greatest Players Of The ABA

The ABA lived from 1967 to 1976. It was a short, chaotic, but also exuberant and fantastic era.

The management in the ABA was generally horrible, but there were some brilliant coaches and some unbelievable players.

Julius Erving was, is, and always will be the face of the ABA, but the league had some other immense talents. Guys like David Thompson, Spencer Haywood, Rick Barry (who started in the NBA but spent several years in the upstart league), Connie Hawkins, Dan Issel, Artis Gilmore, George Gervin, and the first great three-point shooter Louie Dampier, come to mind, along with Charlie Scott and Mel Daniels.

This video looks at some of the best players in ABA history.

The league was a race between chaos and brilliance. The merger with the NBA happened in 1976, just in time, because of mismanagement and the lack of a TV contract; the ABA was on borrowed time.

It's really too bad the league couldn't sustain itself, but it changed basketball forever, and its legacy should not be forgotten.

#2900. Crude oil prices fall below $90 a barrel as Vance blames GOP election defeat on Iran war

Crude oil prices tumbled Wednesday after Vice President JD Vance blamed the war against Iran for Republican midterm election reversals.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate lost 8.3% to close at $88.12 a barrel. Brent crude futures, the international benchmark, shed 8.7% to settle at $94.62.

"The reason we lost is the war," Vance said in a social media post Wednesday. "The president himself said so last night."

Democrats swept the House and Senate in Tuesday's elections, giving the party leverage to pressure President Donald Trump to end the unpopular war that has spiked gasoline and diesel prices.

"The elections are leading the market to conclude that Trump might now be inclined to make a deal," said Andy Lipow, president of Lipow Oil Associates.

The Democrats may try to restrict funding for the war, Lipow said. Trump's announcement earlier this week that the U.S. will cut some funding to Israel could force Jerusalem to accept Washington's decisions, he said.

Trump told the Financial Times on Tuesday that he has no immediate plans to strike Iran. The oil market had priced in the risk of a major escalation after the elections, gaining more than 10% in October.

Oil futures have fallen more than 14% this week as senior U.S. and Iranian officials indicated they're open to a diplomatic settlement of the conflict, which has disrupted global energy supplies for eight months.

The sell-off "could be getting a bit overdone," said Rebecca Babin, senior energy trader at CIBC. The market is in a "shoot first, ask questions" later mode as emotions run high after the unexpected election results, Babin said.

Many observers expected a Democrat tsunami could push Trump to escalate with Iran, Babin said. "Vance's commentary and the public lack of backing from key allies have challenged that assumption," she said.

But the odds still favor military escalation in the region, warned Bob McNally, president of Rapidan Energy and a former advisor to President George W. Bush.

"Iran is hardened in its uncompromising position," McNally said. "Trump, having now paid a huge price for his determination to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon, is unlikely to let go of this legacy goal and walk away."
