#2895. Is Mitchell Robinson washed? (and other Mailbag answers)

Ok, for full disclosure, the question in the title wasn't actually asked. But that is the kind of vibe this mailbag has, so I felt it was representative of the overall feel. And as a reminder, I did ask people to provide their hot takes and questions. So don't be mad that people delivered. Don't hate the playa, hate the game. Which, would honestly be fair in this case.

Hot take 1: Big Country looks out of shape. Over the first few games he's playing well below how he has performed in prior seasons. I will be interested to see if this continues or if this is a product of the team getting used to Big Mitch and him getting used to the team.

The boring/optimistic answer is to preach patience and give him time to acclimate to his new surroundings and role. The concern is that these things don't always work out as we would hope. He had a specific role in New York and thrived in that system. What if he doesn't work within the Celtics structure? We haven't even hit the first time missed due to injury that we all expected.

At the moment I'm not overly worried (once again, small sample size) but it is something to keep an eye on.

Hot take 2: Derrick White should be traded. There are many trade approaches that could be taken, his contract could be used to grab either another center or forward in an effort to lean into Peyton, Baylor, Hugo, and Ron stepping up to fill his role/shift the ball handler role more onto JT and PG13, the other would be to find a way to make a trade exception, have the aforementioned bench players absorb some of those minutes and also create minutes for Chris Cenac. Ultimately Derrick White is close to his athletic downturn. His playstyle, though not nearly as reliant on his athletic ability, does operate on the right side of a thin margin that contributes of his ability to stay in front, react defensively, and generally use what athletic ability he does have to keep up with the more athletic players he usually is tasked with guarding. Losing a half step, which I believe he is in the process of doing, will put him on the WRONG side of that margin and we need to deal him before he is on the wrong side of that margin.

I love, love, love Derrick White. I do not want to trade Derrick White. I will fight for Derrick White.

With all that said, it is hard to argue that he might be a logical option to move. Not necessarily this year, but perhaps by this coming offseason. Smart GM's tend to move on from players before they lose a step and White's shooting woes last year indicate that he might be close to that downturn you are talking about.

He has a large (but not max) contract that could be paired with picks or players to bring back another star or an impact long-term piece.

With all that said, I'd be very reluctant to move White until one or two of the younger players has firmly established themselves as consistent performers. Not just "shows us something occasionally" but contributing on both ends on a nightly basis. You need consistent, effective adults on the floor to win games.

Hot take 3: we should flip Garza and give his minutes to Chris Cenac. Right now Garza is the much better player by a decent margin. I agree that the current minute allocation where Garza is the third big in the rotation will lead to the most wins throughout the season. However, Chris and Luka fill very similar niches when they are on the floor. Giving Chris those minutes now will lead to more winning further down the line. The one drawback is that Cenac starts to click early which will increase his value when his rookie contract ends which may cause an unnecessary increase in his first contract negotiations.

I legitimately don't think either of those last two things will happen, but for the first one I am optimistic that we will see an improvement of Mitch's fit within the next few games…

I support the idea of finding minutes for Cenac, but I don't think we necessarily need to bench Garza to get them. First of all, I think he could use the reps in Maine. Secondly, I think there will be plenty of scheduled (or sometimes unscheduled) games off for Mitchell or Queta and there will be opportunities for Cenac to play on the 2nd night of a back-t0-back or in garbage time.

I should probably be writing in all caps, cus that's how people seem to talk on ESPN, but I'll restrain myself.

Hot take 1: Luka Garza is the Celtics best offensive rebounder.

Hot take 2: Mitchell Robinson retires mid-season and joins Monster Jam, a place where his truck actually fits in the parking garage.

I wonder if Joe has had any thoughts about "borrowing" the truck to use as a getaway vehicle for his next theoretical ballpark heist.

Queta earned the starting spot last year, and Mitch is did not come in prepared to take over the starting spot. Queta should play the start and ends of the quarters, staggering his rest to be able to play 24-30 mpg. Mitch should play shorter stints in the middle of quarters to about 18-24 mpg; and Luka plays as needed. Refs are morons and they have a quicker whistle for backups. Let Queta's experience and reputation grow as a starter.

Mitch's minutes are about the same, points steady, rebounds way down, fouls up, blocks down. He's a career backup that should be beating up on other backups. Nobody is doing hack-a-mitch at the beginning of quarters. He's not playing like a starter, and Queta is thrown into a completely different environment from where he was a steady performer last year.

I will admit, I haven't had a strong take on which big should start. I can see the argument for both of them. Both of them are what I would consider starting quality bigs (based on performance in previous seasons). Both could and should thrive in a bench role. Both should get about the same number of minutes.

The rest is just about fit and chemistry with the units that they play with. I tend to think that the worries about hack-a-Mitch are a bit over-stated. It seems more important to me that he knows where to be on the court and how his teammates are going to be delivering the ball (or how their shots come off the rim). That will take time to develop and the coaching staff is going to need to see what downstream impacts occur in various lineup combinations.

Hmm, that answer seems pretty boring. Is this where I should suggest that he's washed and the team should trade him as soon as possible (December 15th I believe)? Asking for a friend.

The Celtics will continue to win exactly 3 out of every 4 games and finish the season with 61.5 wins, including the first tie in NBA history.

Now I'm trying to think of the scenario that would result in a tie. Maybe both teams would have to foul out all their players with the score tied. In theory that could happen if the team's got into a benches clearing brawl right off the tipoff (not that I'm rooting for that).

Oh, what if Tatum and Brown decide to play a full game of 1 on 1, telling all their teammates to clear out (you know, like they typically did in clutch time for years). Each one pounds the ball for 20 seconds, takes a side-step 3 pointer that clangs off the rim, the other one rebounds, and repeats the process. Game ends in a 26-26 tie (on 20% shooting efficiency) and everyone agrees that the game should not continue into overtime for the sake of all our sanities.

Both hug and dap up after the game and go out for a dinner of lamb chops and gravy.

i would like to see mike conley get some floor time. looked good the only time he played.

Speaking as someone who is, …umm, matured past optimal playing conditions, there are going to be days when he looks as good as his best days in the league (or at least close). But there are also going to be days when he feels like he just got run over by Mitchell Robinson's truck.

I really love the way that Joe Mazzulla is giving him a chance to play the role of player-coach. Between that and giving his assistant coaches opportunities to shine in preseason and elsewhere, Joe is developing a positive reputation. One that could lead to future players in the waning years signing on to get that kind of coaching internship experience. And when the current assistant coaches move on to greener head coaching pastures, more will be excited about joining the program.

Two ideas: (1) All due respect to Red_Auerbach_Junior, will Luka Garza take us to the promised land on sheer effort? and (2) will this be the year the Maine Celtics take the G League championship?

You laugh about Garza and his piston pumping arms when he runs, but every single banner in the rafters included contributions from unsung, try-hards that gave it their all. Sometimes it just takes that one 50-50 ball going to the Celtics because someone just flat-out wanted it more. Give him a Tommy Point!

The Maine Red Claw Celtics are going to have a stacked roster, no doubt. The one thing that could hurt them is the Celtics load-managing the parent team and calling up the best players. But it should be fun most nights.

Will some fans stop claiming that the new owners are cheap after they just donated $200 million to Boston Children's Hospital?

Seriously though, I get the frustration. Celtics fans have had to watch their title winning team get dismantled piece by piece. Most understand the difference between being "cheap" and working within the strict confines of the aprons, but it is still a tough pill to swallow.

At some point, Brad Stevens is going to bring them a deal that cashes in on the "optionality" and asks them to pay well into the tax. He knows that it will have to be the right deal and he can only play that card a few times. So far, he's proven he's a lot smarter than most GMs at this. Will the ownership group agree? We'll see, but I'm hopeful.

The Celtics will defeat the Grizzlies to win the NBA cup with Boston sitting it's top 9 players to save the from injury. They will not hang the banner.

The Celtics "stay ready" group would absolutely give it their all and make for an entertaining Cup game. Still not sure if I'm ready to be a "cup respecter" or whatever the kids are calling it, but hey, it is something to talk about. So why not try to win it?

Here's a non-Celtics one. In order to increase the teams' giddiness, the Timberwolves will trade for Josh Giddey

Is Paul George the partner and mentor that Tatum has always needed?

Tatum has had plenty of positive mentors over the years (Al Horford, Marcus Smart, Jrue Holiday, etc.). And having Jaylen Brown on his team had a lot of very real positives (like a ring, for example). But it is no secret that Tatum idolized George growing up and patterns a lot of his game off the vet.

Makes you wonder what might have happened if Tatum was paired with him earlier in his career (preferably during a healthy stretch). but I guess we'll never know.

I will also point out that replacing Brown would not be an easy gig for most players on the downswing of their careers. Thus far, George has handled himself exceptionally well and deserves the respect he's getting.

Is Walsh only Boston's 4th or 5th best prospect?

First of all, it is worth noting that Walsh at 22 is a year younger than rookie Dillon Mitchell. Which should put some perspective on his development.

Is Jayson Tatum going to be able to come off the bench to allow Amari Williams to develop as a starter?

I'm starting to think that Amari is the center version of JD Davison. Too good for G-League, not quite good enough for a regular role in the NBA. But I've been wrong before.

What are the chances that PP makes the All Star team?

He needs to be a little more consistent night-in-night-out against the league's best defenses, but he has that kind of potential. Might also take a few stat-padding games early in the season to boost his numbers and chances.

Do you think RH, Jr. will see the light of day this year? And something I've always wondered about: Why are some carrots oranger than an orange?

Hey man, there will be load management games where the stay ready guys will have to stay readier than ready.

I also think there could be another 2 week stretch where Harper is the soup du jour on Joe's menu.

Not sure about carrots, but have you ever seen Eminem explain how it is possible to rhyme the word "orange?" Amazing stuff.

Sorry, Jeff. But I wouldn't pretend to be Perkins if you paid me!

Fair enough! Thanks for the hot takes and questions everybody!

#2894. Democrats take Congress in a blue wave. Here's what it means for your money

With Democrats flipping control of Congress in the midterm election, the shift in power on Capitol Hill could reshape household budgets — from student loan bills and health insurance premiums to housing costs and taxes.

Experts say results show that rising costs were top of mind for voters.

"I see this election as a repudiation of the anti-affordability agenda from the Trump administration," said Daniel Bunn, president and CEO of the Tax Foundation, a nonpartisan, nonprofit research organization.

"Whether that's the conflict in the Middle East or the tariffs, just top to bottom, it's clear to me that was a real concern of the electorate," he said.

White House spokesman Matthew Middleton said in an emailed statement that the Trump administration has delivered "tangible wins to make life more affordable for American families." Officials "will continue to put hardworking American's first with or without support from Democrats," he wrote.

Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and Senate, which could make it easier for the party to advance legislation. They can also use hearings and investigations to press federal agencies and companies to answer for policies and practices that impact consumers, and exert leverage over confirmation of President Donald Trump's nominees.

But turning campaign promises into changes people feel in their finances won't be easy. Trump can veto bills a Democrat-controlled Congress passes, limiting how much of their agenda could become law. Based on projections, Democrats will lack the numbers needed to override a presidential veto.

Any changes will also take time: Newly elected members of Congress won't take office until January.

Here are eight ways Democrats’ new congressional majority could affect your money.

"Not surprisingly, the Dems are going to be keyed in on affordability with respect to healthcare," said Zach Gaumer, vice president of health policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonpartisan think tank.

OBBBA made Trump's 2017 tax cuts permanent and added temporary tax breaks. These included new deductions for tip income, overtime earnings, seniors and auto loan interest, which will expire after 2028 without an extension.

Democrats may support extending these provisions. But as the Democrat-controlled Congress maps out its agenda, lawmakers will have to address the federal budget deficit. For fiscal year 2026, the budget deficit ballooned to nearly $2 trillion, the Treasury announced in October.

Newly elected Democratic senators James Talarico of Texas, Troy Jackson of Maine and Annie Andrews of South Carolina all campaigned on expanding the child tax credit. Earlier this year, Senate Democrats also floated ideas to expand the standard deduction and create income tax exemptions for lower-earning Americans — which are costly proposals, according to Bunn from the Tax Foundation.

"I've called these termites in the tax code," he said. "You cannot consistently narrow your base, especially for the income tax, and expect your spending priorities to be well-funded."

With Democrats in control of Congress, they are likely to hold hearings and subpoena Trump officials over the administration's student loan policies, said higher education expert Mark Kantrowitz.

One priority may be pressing the Education Department to fix the popular Public Service Loan Forgiveness program, which cancels the remaining federal student debt of eligible government and nonprofit workers after a decade. Over the summer, Trump officials reassessed repayment timelines, delaying some borrowers' forgiveness date by years.

Broad student loan forgiveness is unlikely: Trump could veto such a bill, and the Supreme Court blocked the Biden administration's plan. More attainable changes could include restoring tax-free treatment of forgiven student debt, raising the new borrowing caps and expanding repayment options — measures that may attract bipartisan support, Kantrowitz said.

Trump has also said he may support legislation capping federal student loan interest rates, a proposal that could give Democrats common ground in their push to ease borrowers’ costs.

Housing affordability was a key election issue, and Democrats will likely explore ways to deliver relief, such as introducing measures to help first-time homebuyers, according to an Oct. 30 analysis by Desjardins, a Canadian financial services company.

However, lawmakers generally have little direct impact on home prices, which are based on market conditions. Mortgage rates, too, are influenced by forces generally beyond congressional control. The yield on the 10-year Treasury, which remains higher than it was before the Iran war — due at least partly to concerns about inflation eroding the value of those bonds — influences mortgage rates.

"Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run," plus more housing supply and a "more dynamic labor market in the long run," said Jake Krimmel, senior economist at Realtor.com.

The bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which automatically became law July 11 without the president's signature due to a constitutional provision, is "a model for what can pass in a divided Washington," Krimmel said. It includes measures to encourage housing construction, reduce regulatory barriers, expand financing options and limit purchases of single-family homes by large institutional investors.

The incoming class of senators will have to address Social Security insolvency.

The program's trustees project that the trust fund Social Security relies on to help pay retirement benefits, formally known as the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund, may be depleted in the fourth quarter of 2032. Without action from Congress before then, benefits could face an across-the-board cut of 22%.

A number of Democratic lawmakers and some Republicans have called for increased payroll taxes on high earners to help shore up Social Security.

A potential SECURE 3.0 bill — a follow-up to bipartisan retirement-savings laws passed in 2019 and 2022 — could help expand workers' access to workplace retirement plans like 401(k)s, for example, among a roster of other reforms, experts said.

"Trump promised to cap credit card rates at 10%," said Aaron Klein, a senior fellow in economic studies at the Brookings Institution, a think tank. "A Democratic Congress might be willing to do that."

Any such cap is likely to be closer to 36% than to the 10% cap Trump has sought, Jaret Seiberg, a financial services policy analyst at TD Cowen, an investment bank, wrote in a research note Wednesday. Lawmakers in the current Congress have proposed legislation with these thresholds.

A cap would likely be welcome news for low- and middle-income Americans, Klein said. They are more likely than wealthy households to use debt to finance their lives and to carry a credit card balance — and therefore owe interest on their charges, he said.

Cardholders might see other impacts of such a policy, such as lower credit limits and a clawback of certain card rewards, he said.

Democrats' takeover of Congress could stall efforts to put crypto-friendly regulations into federal law, but everyday investors shouldn't expect much to change immediately.

"Platforms will keep offering the products they offer today, and they still have friendly regulators at the SEC and CFTC who are approving an ever-wider range of crypto products," said Lee Reiners, a cryptocurrency regulation expert and lecturing fellow of economics at Duke University.

The election results could threaten the Clarity Act, which would give the Commodity Futures Trading Commission greater authority over cryptocurrency trading while reducing the Securities and Exchange Commission's role. The bill passed the House in July 2025 by a 294-134 vote, but failed to advance in the Senate in September on a 49-50 procedural vote. "CLARITY is dead," said Reiners, adding that he sees little chance of the bill passing during the lame-duck session before Democrats take control in January.

Any future legislation aimed at creating a regulatory framework for digital assets would likely need to address Democrats' concerns about investor protections and conflicts of interest involving Trump's crypto businesses, said Martin Mulyadi, an accounting professor at Shenandoah University.

The SEC and CFTC can also continue developing crypto-friendly rules without Congress. But those rules are easier for a future administration to reverse than legislation, said Marcel Miu, a certified financial planner and founder of Simplify Wealth Planning based in Austin, Texas. "I'd tell investors to size their crypto investments as if the rules could change repeatedly over the coming years," Miu said.

The Iran war has led oil prices — and those of gasoline, jet fuel, heating oil, diesel and other fuels refined from oil — to surge. That has trickled into higher prices across the U.S. economy, experts said.

Oil prices fell on Wednesday on hopes that the election outcome will push Trump to make a deal to end the war.

"Ending the war in Iran would be the biggest policy lever to reducing inflation," the Brookings Institution's Klein said.

A Democrat-led Congress could try to limit Trump's ability to continue the war, perhaps by cutting funding.

"Congress' biggest power is the power of the purse," Klein said. "Wars are expensive."

— CNBC's Lorie Konish and Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting.

#2893. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2892. The most terrifying part of losing is having nothing to gain

We currently find ourselves in the trenches, five games into the season, with the Phoenix Suns sitting at 1-4. And when you're down in the trenches, you typically don't have the luxury of looking at things from 30,000 feet. There's too much happening around you, and the moment you start thinking too big or too far into the future, something changes that demands your immediate attention. Such is life. Ultimately, what defines quality strategy is the ability to balance the immediate challenges in front of you with the health and viability of your franchise over the long term.

But there's a realization slowly creeping into the frontal lobes of our brains, something we've been actively trying to avoid thinking about because we know that once we accept it as reality, the outlook becomes increasingly frustrating and dystopian. Last year's team saved us from these thoughts. This year's team may not be able to. The Suns don't own their own draft capital, and no matter what happens, sucking as a basketball team provides them absolutely no benefit.

The reality of professional sports is that teams ebb and flow. The advantage of possessing your own draft assets is that when an inevitable down season arrives, you have an opportunity to turn that adversity into something useful. Down seasons can happen for a multitude of reasons, with injuries often being the primary culprit. You struggle for a year, acquire a valuable asset through the draft, and get back to work. If you're a perpetually bad team, the draft provides a pathway toward rebuilding your roster.

There's no perfect formula for constructing a championship roster, or even one that's consistently competitive. Throughout NBA history, organizations have employed numerous strategies with varying degrees of success. But as we look at the season ahead, we're staring at a Suns team that appears disjointed and poorly constructed. The roster features expensive players with duplicative skill sets, an abundance of undersized contributors, and injuries that only complicate matters further.

Things can get really bad in Phoenix, really quickly. The most unsettling part? There's no eject button.

You might believe that trading Devin Booker is ultimately the answer, but it's not. Perhaps Booker is overvalued as an asset within the Phoenix market because of everything he's meant to this organization and how long he's been its centerpiece. His value on the trade market may ultimately produce a return that falls short of what Suns fans envision. If that return includes multiple first-round picks, there's a very real possibility those selections end up being far less desirable than what Phoenix would need to meaningfully rebuild over the long term.

Booker is now 30, which isn't what you would classify as "old". At least, you wouldn't have 10 years ago. Kirk Golderberry of The Ringer, however, recently delved into the decline in players over 30 and their impact on the game:

During the 2025-26 regular season, players 30 and older made up just 17.5 percent of the NBA. That's the lowest share in at least 30 years. Meanwhile, for the seventh straight season, more than half the league (54.6 percent) was 25 or younger. In 1997-98, that number was just 36 percent.

Yeah, that wasn't exactly my favorite read of the week. Nothing quite like being reminded that the guy who's supposed to be in his prime and leading this team to victories is rapidly becoming antiquated in a league that increasingly values youth and durability over sustainability and longevity. Great. Fantastic. Love that for us.

I had someone suggest trading Devin Booker to the Detroit Pistons for Ausar Thompson and four first-round picks. As unlikely as that scenario might be, let's play it out.

The Suns acquire Ausar Thompson, meaning they're now building around Jalen Green, Thompson, Dillon Brooks, Miles Bridges, and Khaman Maluach. How many games is that team winning? My guess is they're a .500 team at best. Let's say, in a best-case scenario, Phoenix somehow ends up with the sixth seed in the Western Conference, although reality probably suggests they're a Play-In team at best. Regardless, because the Suns don't control their own draft pick, they're handing that selection over to Houston. No, the Suns get the worst pick between Cleveland, Minnesota, and Utah. For thought exercise purposes, let's say Phoenix receives the 25th overall selection.

But wait! You traded Devin Booker to Detroit for four first-round picks! Except now Booker is playing alongside Cade Cunningham and Jalen Duren, and suddenly the Pistons look fantastic because Booker is operating in a role that better suits him. Detroit hypothetically ends up with the 24th pick.

So Phoenix walks into next year's draft holding the 24th and 25th overall selections. Cool. No high-end prospects. No obvious game changers. Instead, you're selecting a pair of developmental projects, hoping they possess enough transferable skills to become viable rotation players three years down the road. You get a couple of Ryan Dunns.

Then it becomes rinse and repeat. Every year, the Suns are struggling to assemble a competitive roster, attempting to do so with Detroit's draft capital while their own selections remain outside their control.

Why am I bringing this up five games into the season? Why am I looking at the bigger picture when I should be focusing on Portland tonight? I think it's because the potential worst-case scenario is beginning to unfold before our eyes. And while the fan base desperately rushes to trade machines, searching for some magical combination of transactions that will fix everything, the reality is becoming increasingly difficult to ignore. This is a very hard situation to fix.

Perhaps it's because I'm seeing the suggestions people are putting out there, and while I understand the strategy behind many of these proposed deals, I ultimately find myself asking one question. How the hell did we get here?

To me, the answer is simple: Bradley Beal.

I know plenty of people will argue that acquiring Kevin Durant was the beginning of the downfall of the Phoenix Suns, and I don't completely disagree. That move was a step in the wrong direction, one that could ultimately take this organization a decade to recover from. The Suns had what makes teams successful in today's NBA: youth, connectivity, chemistry, and a veteran presence to stabilize it all. It's reminiscent of the Seven Seconds or Less Suns, a team that was ahead of its time. By the time the rest of the league caught up to that philosophy, Phoenix had already moved on to something different, never fully capitalizing on the strategy it helped pioneer.

Through that lens, I can accept the argument that Durant's acquisition was the root cause of where we find ourselves today. As for the extracurricular drama he brought to the organization, and the notion that his presence was ultimately more detrimental than productive, I politely disagree. Sure, there were extracurriculars with Kevin Durant. Nobody knows that better than I do. But he was also an elite scorer, someone you could rely on at virtually any moment to get you a bucket. It was the moves made around him that ultimately sunk this team, and none was more damaging than acquiring Bradley Beal.

The Suns acquired a depreciating asset whose skill set was largely duplicative of Devin Booker's, sacrificed the remainder of their available draft capital to do so, and inherited a no-trade clause that made an already difficult situation even worse. Ultimately, they had to buy Beal out and stretch the financial consequences across multiple seasons.

Think about this team right now and how differently you might view it with an additional $19.3 million in payroll flexibility. Need a point guard? How about another big man who can actually rebound? That money could help address those issues, but it's unavailable because the Suns waived Bradley Beal. And the ancillary draft capital that could facilitate additional transactions? That's gone, too, because they shipped it away to acquire Bradley fucking Beal.

That's the deal we're going to continue paying for through 2030. That's the transaction whose consequences continue to suffocate this franchise, even after the player himself is long gone. And that's the draft capital we sacrificed, capital that could have been used to facilitate the very transactions this team so desperately needs today.

The reality of what the Phoenix Suns might become if they aren't a competitive basketball team is something we accepted at the beginning of last season. The thought process at the time was that eventually we'd trade Grayson Allen and Royce O'Neale, Dillon Brooks was a throw-in from the Kevin Durant trade, and Jalen Green had enough upside to warrant seeing what he could become. Expectations were low as we prepared for this dommsdat scenario to play out. Phoenix surprised us, however, winning 45 games.

The team did eventually trade Grayson Allen and Royce O'Neale, and in return acquired Miles Bridges, who thus far has probably been the most consistent player on the team. But we also surrendered our 2033 first-round pick to make it happen. Why? Nobody fucking knows. Because the Suns "expect to be good", right? They operate as though they'll never need a contingency plan, and when circumstances inevitably demand one, they're screwed.

Jalen Green has upside. He has athleticism. And he absolutely does not fit on this team. If he were making $16 million a year, perhaps I wouldn't think much of it. But he's making $36.3 million this season and another $36 million next year, with a player option he's almost certainly going to exercise because he's unlikely to command that kind of money on the open market. So the Suns are left holding the bag. Again.

When was the last time the Suns made a trade and actually won it? When was the last time you looked back three years after a transaction and thought, you know what, "Slam dunk for Phoenix"? The Barkley trade? KJ?

That's why I'm feeling so much doom and gloom right now. That's where the frustration comes from. The reality we managed to put off last season because the team overperformed is beginning to show signs of coming to fruition this year. Now another year has passed, Devin Booker's trade value isn't getting any higher, and even if it were, it wouldn't change the fundamental problem. This franchise simply cannot benefit from being a losing basketball team.

I'm writing this because I see all the solutions people are desperately trying to come up with to save the Suns, and every time another proposal surfaces, I find myself asking the same questions: How? Why? Is Dejounte Murray going to be the guy who saves this franchise? Domantas Sabonis? Are those really the answers to the problems we're facing? No.

The fact that we've reached the point of entertaining those possibilities only five games into the season says plenty about the long-term outlook of this organization. And perhaps that's what I'm really doing here. Trying to mentally prepare myself for a reality we've spent the past year hoping we'd never have to confront.

The Suns can improve their execution, find better lineup combinations, and perhaps salvage something from this season. But none of that changes the underlying reality. This organization has sacrificed its flexibility, its financial resources, and its ability to benefit from failure. Five games into the season, we're already contemplating the consequences of decisions made years ago. The Suns aren't necessarily doomed to lose. They're trapped in a situation where losing doesn't help them win tomorrow.

#2891. What Wall Street billionaires are saying about Democrats' midterm sweep

America's billionaires woke up Wednesday to a political reckoning — and a Wall Street rally.

As Democrats celebrated a sweeping midterm victory, some of the country's wealthiest donors traded barbs over record election spending. Others warned the results could threaten the artificial intelligence boom.

Wall Street, however, cheered the outcome. The Dow Jones Industrial Average soared 1,569 points, or 3%, while the S&P 500 gained 1.6% and the Nasdaq Composite rose 1.4%, as investors welcomed political gridlock, reduced uncertainty and President Donald Trump's willingness to accept the results.

Virgin Group founder Richard Branson took aim at the billions spent influencing the election.

"I have felt embarrassed by the billions that tech entrepreneurs and hedge fund managers have spent," Branson wrote Wednesday on LinkedIn, calling for campaign finance reform. Branson did not give donations that met the threshold reporting in the 2026 election cycle.

His comments came as Elon Musk, the election's largest individual donor, watched his preferred Republicans lose the Texas Senate and governor races despite pouring $224 million into the state — nearly half his record $474 million in political giving nationwide, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records.

Early Wednesday, the Tesla and SpaceX CEO suggested on X that data centers in space were looking increasingly appealing before joking that his "election-interfering space lasers" had failed.

LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, another major political donor, took a sharper tone.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote late Tuesday on X, accusing Musk of spending "outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power" while defending his own contributions to Democrats.

Other Democratic megadonors celebrated. "The world changed yesterday," Alex Soros wrote Wednesday on X, hailing the results as a generational victory after his family contributed roughly $103 million to Democratic political efforts.

But beyond the political sparring, some of the country's richest investors were focused on a potentially bigger consequence of the Democratic sweep: the future of the AI boom.

Even before polls closed Tuesday, Pershing Square founder Bill Ackman warned on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that slowing AI development could jeopardize America's technological edge. "Practically speaking, we can't slow down," he said.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, billionaire investor Chamath Palihapitiya sounded an even starker warning.

Palihapitiya initially questioned whether the early results would hold, but warned that if they did, the implications for the AI trade could be severe.

"And the only way to sink the AI trade is to sink the data centers," Palihapitiya said during an election-night livestream of the "All-In" podcast.

Texas offered an early test of those fears. Democratic Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa, who has proposed a moratorium on new data-center construction until stronger safeguards are established, defeated three-term Republican incumbent Greg Abbott.

During the same livestream, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner warned the Democratic wave could mean at least 10 to 15 gigawatts of expected AI data-center capacity would not come online in 2027. Wall Street analyst Dan Ives estimated Wednesday that 10% of planned U.S. data centers would no longer go ahead.

AMD CEO Lisa Su acknowledged the growing resistance to AI's expansion, urging technology companies to demonstrate the benefits of data centers to local communities.

"At the end of the day, what we need to do is ensure that everybody wins from AI," Su told CNBC Wednesday.

Yet Wall Street may have found opportunity in the same slowdown that worried AI's biggest backers.

Fewer new data centers could increase the value of existing facilities while easing the enormous spending burden on technology giants. Big Tech stocks surged Wednesday, adding more than $750 billion in market value at one point, even as smaller, debt-dependent data-center operators tumbled.

#2890. 15 players to watch amid NFL trade deadline buzz

Take a look at the NFL standings. There's no sure thing this season.

Once the Los Angeles Rams got off to a rocky start, it opened things up around the league. There is no runaway team this season, and there is also a group of teams already looking ahead to 2027. That could lead to a ton of activity before the NFL's trade deadline, which is Nov. 10. There has been plenty of action well ahead of the deadline, with the most recent deal involving the Arizona Cardinals sending recently demoted quarterback Jacoby Brissett to the QB-starved Baltimore Ravens.

Before that players like guard Quenton Nelson went to Seattle, defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons landed in Chicago, defensive tackle DeForest Buckner headed to San Francisco, cornerback Joey Porter Jr. was shipped to Dallas and receiver Marvin Harrison Jr. being was dealt to Houston.

That's already a lot of big trades and we still have plenty of time before Tuesday's deadline. Here are the 15 names to watch between now and the deadline:

If we take the Browns at their reported word, Sanders is not likely to be traded at the deadline. You can never say never until the deadline passes, but for now it appears Cleveland will continue to carry four quarterbacks, which includes Sanders. He has been QB2 behind Deshaun Watson and has replaced Watson for a handful of snaps after Watson got hurt. Watson was sacked eight times Sunday, and the 5-3 Browns might fear a more serious Watson injury as they sit just outside the current playoff picture after Week 8's defeat to the Steelers.

Given his cost-controlled salary, low draft pedigree and Cleveland's crowded QB room, Sanders might normally be considered a higher-likelihood trade candidate. So far there has been little apparent traction. We'll see if that changes over the next week. Perhaps there's even a world where Sanders is in the mix to start in Cleveland if the free-agent-to-be Watson is not brought back in 2027. – Eric Edholm

The Eagles reportedly won't trade Barkley. That also doesn't make much sense. Philadelphia is going nowhere, Barkley is turning 30 years old in February, his production has declined since 2024 and the Eagles have never been overly sentimental about player moves. Barkley brought great memories and was the superstar on a Super Bowl champion, but the Eagles should listen to calls. They could pass if teams try to get Barkley cheap, given his current injuries, but it seems like he'd have more value to a contending team this season than the Eagles going forward.

Maybe the Eagles are serious and Barkley is indeed off the table. That won't stop speculation. It makes a ton of sense for Philadelphia to move Barkley now. That's why we're still keeping an eye on Barkley as the deadline approaches. If they don't move him, that decision will be discussed not only at the deadline but beyond. – Frank Schwab

It would be a surprise if Taylor isn't traded, after the Colts sent off guard Quenton Nelson and defensive tackle DeForest Buckner. The question is who lands Taylor and his 8,208 career rushing yards. He's still one of the NFL's best runners and plenty of teams have a need. The Giants, Broncos, Packers and Vikings top the list. While other backs are likely available, nobody else would pop like Taylor. Other players might be more prudent from a football aspect (Jeffery Simmons to the Bears, most notably) but no player acquisition would generate more buzz than nabbing Taylor. – Frank Schwab

Pollard has said he doesn't want to be traded, and the Titans reportedly have rebuffed offers, but a 1-7 record and a reasonable proposal could change their minds. He's in the final year of a three-year deal, makes fairly modest starting RB money and would provide the Titans with some salary cap relief if they moved him.

Pollard has averaged a robust 4.5 yards per carry, has back-to-back 100-yard rushing outings (and five TDs in two games) and remains in his relative prime at age 29 with fewer than 2,000 career touches. On paper, he checks every box for a sale candidate if the Titans want to add more draft assets before the deadline. And for teams looking for RB help, he might come far cheaper than some of the other players mentioned above. – Eric Edholm

The memories of Thomas' rookie season might last forever. They could lead to some team thinking it can revive his career. Thomas was a first-round draft pick in 2024 and had 1,282 yards and 10 touchdowns. He looked like a future star. In the season and a half since he has fewer than 1,000 yards. He has fallen well out of the Jaguars' hierarchy at receiver, leading to plenty of trade speculation. The Jaguars, who look like Super Bowl contenders, could keep Thomas just in case there are injuries at receiver. But teams like the Seahawks, Chiefs or Commanders would likely be interested, hoping Thomas rediscovers his rookie form in a new situation. – Frank Schwab

Interestingly, Jennings was rumored to be a target of the 49ers, trying to bring him back into the fold, but declined the offer, saying he didn't want to go back to San Francisco. That's especially interesting considering his best production came with the 49ers, notching a 975-yard season in 2024 and a nine-TD campaign a year ago before leaving on a one-year deal in Minnesota. Jennings has been almost a ghost with the Vikings, with only six catches in his six games and only modest upticks in his activity lately.

The Vikings need all the offense they can find, but Jennings hasn't been a great fit. If they can include him in a deal for a running back or defensive depth, the Vikings might consider it. – Eric Edholm

Downs has requested a trade, saying he's "open to whatever" when it comes to moving on from the Colts. The team has already kick-started its offloading efforts with Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner, and don't appear to have strong intentions to keep Downs. The 25-year-old receiver is in the final year of his rookie deal and has stepped up as the Colts' leading option with Alec Pierce hurt. Downs doesn't fit a WR1 profile but could be a quality complement for a team seeking an upgrade to its unit, similar to the Seahawks' deadline trade for Rashid Shaheed last year.

Downs is a smaller slot receiver and might be a fit for only certain teams, but with his trade request and the seeming league-wide need for WR help, he figures to be a candidate who possibly moves on soon. – Eric Edholm

Jeudy has always been mercurial. He came into the NFL as a first-round draft pick and was fine early on with the Broncos, but wore out his welcome and was traded to the Browns. With the Browns, he had a stellar 2024, with 1,229 yards and a Pro Bowl nod. But he has dipped again and with the Browns turning toward rookies Denzel Boston and KC Concepcion, Jeudy has just 171 yards this season. Still, Jeudy has one of the biggest plays of the Browns' 5-3 start, a 33-yard catch against the Ravens to set up a game-winning field goal.

Jeudy, 27, could be an intriguing gamble for a team that needs juice at receiver. He's no sure thing, but the talent is there. – Frank Schwab

Goedert has been consistent. Over the past seven seasons he has posted between 496 and 830 yards each season. He's not a superstar at the position but he is reliable, even at age 31. Goedert is on a one-year deal and it's not like the Eagles are going to be competitive this season. That makes him a perfect trade target, and a few teams could use a solid pass-catching tight end for the stretch run. – Frank Schwab

Mack isn't the same player he was when he was a first-team All-Pro three times in four years. His peak might lead him to the Pro Football Hall of Fame. He's 35 years old now, but still is an effective player. And with the 0-7 Chargers going nowhere, it would make sense for the team to move on and allow Mack a chance at playoff success in what might be the final season of his career. Mack is in his 13th season and has never been part of a playoff win.

Mack would add leadership and a strong edge defender down the stretch for a contender. Mack has 8.5 sacks in 19 games over the past two seasons, including three in seven games this season. Given his age, he might not cost that much. A reunion with the Bears seems like the perfect fit. – Frank Schwab

When the Eagles drafted Smith in 2023, draft experts lost their minds. They gave the Eagles a consensus grade point average of 4.12 — better than an A. While the Eagles' first pick, defensive tackle Jalen Carter, worked out very well, Smith has not.

Smith has just 14.5 career sacks but still has the draft pedigree and enticing athleticism. The Eagles picked up Smith's fifth-year option for 2027, which means they won't be looking to give him away. But for a floundering Eagles team, the idea of recouping some value on Smith during his fourth lukewarm season has to be a possibility.

There are a lot of Eagles on this list. Philadelphia becomes the seller to watch as the trade deadline approaches. For some contender looking for a shot on a sleeper, taking a stab at Smith at age 25 could pay off nicely. – Frank Schwab

Sweat reportedly requested a trade in August, and the Cardinals very publicly said they had no interest in honoring that request. Could they have a different mindset now? Arizona has won two of three and is a fairly respectable 3-5, albeit in a very tough division the Cardinals are unlikely to win. Sweat's play has not been as impactful as a year ago, when he notched 12 sacks and four forced fumbles, but he had a huge sack in overtime of Dak Prescott in Dallas on Sunday.

Speaking of Dallas, the Cowboys figure to be among the teams that could have interest in Sweat, along with the Packers, where Jonathan Gannon (his former coach) is defensive coordinator. Sweat's contract kicks up to $23.6 million salary cap hits each of the next two seasons, which puts him outside the top-10 edge rusher deals, but there have been rumors of Sweat wanting a new deal after this season, which could complicate things. – Eric Edholm

There are conflicting reports on the 25-year-old Ojomo and whether the Eagles might consider dangling him. Normally, they'd want to build around an emerging defensive tackle who has started the past three games and been more productive recently than highly paid DTs Jalen Carter and Jordan Davis. But Ojomo is set to hit free agency, and it's unlikely the Eagles can afford him after what they paid Carter and Davis. If the Eagles have designs on re-signing Ojomo, they'll have to be creative. They are 2-6, losers of six straight, including back-to-back thumpings in prime time, and QB Jalen Hurts (along with others) is on injured reserve. The Jeffery Simmons trade to Chicago had to be an eye-opener for Philly, value-wise, and might cause the team to consider moving him before the deadline. – Eric Edholm

Baker has been tremendous for the Cardinals over his 10 seasons with the team, making eight Pro Bowls. But it might be time for a rebuilding club to move on. Baker can provide playmaking at secondary, as he has for a decade. He is signed through 2027, with a reasonable salary of a little more than $16 million for next season. So he wouldn't be a short-term rental for a team.

It would sting for the Cardinals to move on from Baker, one of the team's best players and only positive lights in the post-Larry Fitzgerald era. But it's prudent for a team looking for the future. And it would be a big get for a contending club that needs a difference maker in the secondary. – Frank Schwab

Following his three-pick game Sunday against Joe Burrow, Hooker is the NFL's INT leader with five, and his 114 return yards off those picks also is first. The 28-year-old veteran has quietly carved out a nice career over eight seasons with the Titans and is enjoying one of his better years, tackling and covering well. The Titans signed him to an extension last summer through the 2028 season with a fairly reasonable salary outside the top 10 safeties.

Given their 1-7 record at the bye, the Titans could offload more vets after trading Jeffery Simmons. – Eric Edholm

#2889. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2888. NASCAR O’Reilly Auto Parts Series Championship Preview: Five Drivers Chase the Title at Homestead

Justin Allgaier enters the championship race as the points leader after finishing runner-up at Martinsville. The veteran has enjoyed one of the best seasons of his career, collecting six victories and 19 top-five finishes. He also has plenty of experience at Homestead, which could give him an advantage over his championship competitors.

Allgaier controls his own destiny and can secure his second career series championship with a strong performance. However, he cannot afford to take his foot off the gas with four drivers close behind.

Love, Day and Kvapil Are Ready to Challenge

Reigning champion Jesse Love sits 22 points behind Allgaier. Love has already won twice during the postseason and knows what it takes to deliver when the championship is on the line. He will attempt to become the first driver since Tyler Reddick to win consecutive titles.

Corey Day is only one point behind Love and enters the finale with plenty of momentum after back-to-back victories at Las Vegas and Charlotte. The Hendrick Motorsports driver has exceeded expectations in his first season and has an opportunity to capture a championship.

Carson Kvapil also remains in contention, sitting 31 points behind the leader. After winning at Martinsville, Kvapil has given himself another opportunity to make history. Sheldon Creed rounds out the championship contenders, trailing by 42 points.

Homestead is known for its multiple racing grooves, making tire management and handling extremely important. Drivers who can run near the wall and maintain speed through the corners could have a major advantage.

With 200 laps available, teams will need to make smart pit-road decisions while avoiding mistakes that could end their championship hopes.

Allgaier has the points advantage, but Love, Day, Kvapil and Creed are not out of the fight. With five drivers still mathematically eligible, Saturday's finale has the potential to deliver an incredible finish to the 2026 season.

#2887. Democrats map out an agenda to address affordability and corruption after midterm sweep

The newly elected Democratic majorities in the Senate and the House are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned, and are preparing to tussle with an antagonistic President Donald Trump.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The legislative push aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to bring to the floor bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law last year.

Democrats — likely led by Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.), who is poised to become the first Black Speaker of the House in U.S. history — also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the Iran war or force Trump to seek congressional authorization to keep it going. Some details of the Democrats' plans had been previously reported by the New York Times.

But Democrats will need to strike a balance between these legislative priorities and a robust oversight and investigatory agenda that the party is mapping out to scrutinize Trump, his family, cabinet and supporters in the business world.

"We haven't evaluated who are going to be the targets of the investigation right out of the gate. We'll have those conversations over the next couple of weeks," Jeffries told CNBC on Wednesday.

"But clearly, things aren't going right for the American people. Capitalism is broken in the way in which it is being experienced by so many working-class Americans and middle-class Americans. We've got to make sure we build an economy, a free market economy, that works for everyday Americans," Jeffries said.

Democrats on the campaign trail for months hammered home a message of affordability. And as corruption emerged as a top issue for voters in the waning months of the election, they tried to connect the Trump administration's perceived bad behavior to Americans' pocketbooks.

Already, prominent Democrats are taking a similar tack as they prepare to assume majorities in both chambers.

Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee, said in a statement on Wednesday that the panel's top priorities "will be affordability for us, accountability for them."

Wyden said the committee would double down on its efforts to make sure billionaires pay their fair share in taxes, address the "corporate chokehold on our nation's health care system" and reassert congressional authority over trade. He also vowed to target the "special interests" that had cozied up to Trump in the past two years.

"From the banks that helped cover up Epstein's crimes, to insurance companies that are delaying and denying health care to churn a profit, to the pharmaceutical company CEOs that price gouge while enjoying Trump's hand-delivered windfalls, when I am chair, they will be forced to answer to the Senate Finance Committee for their role in hurting the American people and enabling Trump's corruption," Wyden said.

Rep. Robert Garcia of California, the top-ranking Democrat on the House Oversight Committee, has vowed to take on Trump's family, corporate corruption, Department of Homeland Security and Immigration and Customs Enforcement overreach and the handling of files related to disgraced sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

Garcia issued a pointed, two-word statement as results tipped toward Democrats on Tuesday night: "Lawyer up."

And Rep. Pramila Jayapal, D-Wash., a former chair of the Congressional Progressive Caucus and member of the House Judiciary and Foreign Affairs committees, said in a recent interview with CNBC that she believed Democrats should target figures like Elon Musk, as well as cabinet secretaries like Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, Secretary of Health and Human Services Robert F. Kennedy Jr., and Attorney General Todd Blanche.

But she acknowledged the challenges that could come with pursuing a large number of potential targets all at once, especially with a potentially unwieldy and ideologically diverse Democratic caucus.

"I think what we should do is pick one or two cases that really exemplify the worst of the corruption, and that we feel we have the most evidence of wrongdoing," Jayapal said. "There are so many things — I think it's important to focus on a couple."

Meanwhile, other Democrats are angling for some kind of ethics reform package that could include prohibitions on stock trading and use of prediction markets for government officials, a federal ban on gerrymandering and limiting super PAC spending.

"My expectation is that when Democrats are the majority, we will pass a strong bill to ban stock trading in Congress, in the executive branch, and beyond, and that this will be part of a larger package of ethics reforms that we pursue," said Rep. Seth Magaziner, D-R.I., a longtime advocate for such legislation.

#2884. Boston Bruins’ NHL-Best Habit Is Fueling Their Hot Start

The Boston Bruins have been one of the NHL's most dangerous teams, especially late in games when opponents have a narrow lead.

No other team in the NHL has scored like the Bruins in the third period. Per EdgeHalla, Boston has averaged 1.50 goals per game in the third period throughout their first 16 contests. Only the St. Louis Blues, at 1.47, have come close.

Their third-period goals-for-to-goals-allowed ratio also paces the league. Boston has scored 24 times in the third frame while allowing just 13 goals.

They are also 2-3-0 when trailing after two periods. While they are under .500 when opponents go into the third frame with the lead, the Bruins still rank first in the NHL in that category.

Boston heats up so much in the third that their 24 third-period goals account for 42.1 percent of their 57 goals scored in 2026-27. That said, Boston isn't that bad in the first period, where they have scored 17 times and allowed 11. Their 17 goals are good for a 1.06 goals-for rate in the first period. That number ranks ninth in the NHL.

While the Bruins typically score first — 7-3-1 when they score before an opponent — they are 4-1-0 when allowing the first goal. Heading into Wednesday night, that is the best mark in the NHL.

If they struggle anywhere, it is in the second period, where they hold a 14-13 goals-for-vs.-goals-against ratio. Their goals-for rate in the second sits at .88. Overall, the number ranks 23rd in the NHL.

That would be a problem if a team struggled in the third period. But the Bruins only need to keep games reasonably close. Should they keep getting hot in the third period, their odds of overcoming third-period deficits should remain relatively favorable.

While it may be logical to claim the 2026-27 season isn't old enough to think that this trend hasn't gained steam, one only needs to look at last season. Boston was at its best in the third period, with a 1.17 goals-for rate. That beat its .99 rate in the first and 1.01 rate in the second.

It may still be early in 2026-27, but the Bruins are showing a year-over-year trend.

In 2025-26, the Bruins were 2-21-3 when trailing after two periods. While they had their highest goals-for rate in the third, it rarely translated into a win.

The biggest change in 2026-27 is that Boston, with their 1.50 goals-for rate in the third, has been changing the narrative and ultimately winning games. After 16 games and trailing in five of those games, the Bruins are on an 84-game pace to enter the third frame trailing in 26 contests — the same number as their 82 regular-season game slate last year.

The difference? They are trending toward winning between 10 and 11 of those games. That puts them on pace to finish 10-16-0 or 11-15-0 when trailing after two. Realistically, some of these games will wind up as overtime losses for Boston. Still, early trends show that Boston should complete more comebacks in 2026-27.

That will make life difficult for their rivals in the Atlantic Division and the rest of the league. The takeaway? It is hard to count out the Bruins this season.

#2883. What was really behind Wednesday's monster rally? Trump quickly making peace

The stock market skyrocketed on Wednesday on the heels of a decisive sweep for Democrats in the 2026 midterm elections. But that was not necessarily the only driver of the climb.

"President Trump's acknowledgement of the Democrats' victory in the midterms and his expressed openness to collaborating with the opposition have helped markets to rally on hopes that contestation of the results and more adverse polarization can be avoided," said Thomas Hempell, Generali Investments senior economist.

Trump told The New York Times and The Wall Street Journal overnight Tuesday that he accepted the results and would work with Democrats. Those headlines coincided with a rebound in stock market futures.

In the lead-up to this year's midterms, some on Wall Street grew increasingly concerned about the prospect of Trump contesting the election results. The Department of Justice's Alaska election lawsuit led to fears over delays with the outcome that would exacerbate the uneasiness in the market that's typically removed once the elections conclude.

CNBC's Fed Survey showed that among 30 fund managers, strategists and economists, 63% believe that the extended uncertainty around the balance of power created by legal challenges to the election results posed "some" market risk. That was a slight increase from 61% in September. Additionally, 14% believed it represented "significant" market risk, which was up from 7%.

Now, investors are breathing a sigh of relief over Trump's acceptance of the results, which should "act as a break on extreme policy outcomes and bring down risk premia," so long as the president's decision remains, said George Saravelos, head of FX Research at Deutsche Bank.

Trump has a history of rallying markets, as well as driving them lower, with his announcements. Jay Woods of Freedom Capital Markets, who noted that the rebound in stocks overnight was partially due to the president's "initial tone and reaction to the results," said the focus will shift to whether Trump's latest move will actually have staying power.

"I think Trump is going to try to do and say the right thing, but at two in the morning, he may change his mind," the chief market strategist said. "He is an X factor."

Woods added that he's "encouraged" by Wednesday's massive rally, but he noted that there are still headwinds that could plague the market over the coming weeks — such as concerns about inflationary pressures as a result of higher energy prices from the conflict in the Middle East.

"How Trump reacts to it will help us navigate those headwinds," he said.

Osaic's Philip Blancato said he believes that if Trump "continues to play nice" with Democrats, the market is going to have a "very strong" end to the year.

Stocks historically tend to rally after the midterms anyway, but this time around, some were worried the solid run-up in the S&P 500 this year was going to potentially hinder those gains. After Trump's reaction, Blancato said he doesn't think the S&P 500 hitting 8,000 by year-end is totally out of the question and that there's "a lot predicated on him, but not completely."

The broad market index gained more than 1% on Wednesday, with its closing price implying a roughly 4% gain to that level.

However, investors should be prepared for any future volatility if Trump's rhetoric does not remain the same, the chief market strategist said.

"The president's always going to be a wildcard," he said. "Let's not let one day make a year. This is an instant reaction to this thing. There's still plenty of things to be hashed out."

#2882. Lions promote LB Joe Bachie to active roster, sign two other LBs

The Detroit Lions announced three linebacker-related roster transactions on Wednesday: signing Joe Bachie from the practice squad to the active roster, signing K.J. Britt from the Patriots' practice squad to the active roster, and signing Owen Pappoe to the practice squad.

No immediate roster moves were necessary, as the Lions previously opened two spots on their 53-man roster on Tuesday when they placed rookie linebacker Jimmy Rolder on injured reserve and waived rookie safety Bishop Fitzgerald. Bachie's promotion from the practice squad opened a spot for Pappoe.

With Bachie operating as the team's LB7, the injuries to Jack Campbell and Rolder have created a need for him to step into a larger role moving forward. As for Britt and Pappoe, it will likely take time for them to acclimate to the Lions' defensive scheme. That said, Britt has plenty of special teams experience, and with the Lions' preference to keep six linebackers active on game days, he could be active as soon as this Sunday against the Dolphins.

Jericho Sims. We saw Sims get his first real minutes against the Wolves, and he was impressive. Was that a blip on the radar, or will we get another impressive performance? #2880. Power-hitter Lee Ivakina continues to be key to UAA volleyball's success as a senior

Nov. 4—Since arriving on campus two years ago as a junior college transfer, Lee Ivakina has been integral to the University of Alaska Anchorage volleyball team's ability to thrive on the court.

In her first season with the team, she led the Seawolves in kills and points and finished third in digs, and she is on track to repeat all three feats heading into the final two weeks of the 2026 regular season.

Through 24 matches played, Ivakina is the only player on the team with 300-plus points and kills. She is averaging career highs with 3.48 kills and 4.03 points per set.

"She's been amazing for us and she's definitely one of our go-getters," head coach Morgan Hooe said. "Coming in as a junior college transfer is pretty tough to jump in and buy into the culture, but Lee has been that standard and kind of kept us that way."

Ivakina isn't the typical transfer student, as she is originally from Varna, Bulgaria.

With help from a recruiting agency back home in Bulgaria, she began her collegiate career at Navarro College, a junior college in Corsicana, Texas.

"After I was done with JUCO, my coaches helped me contact UAA, and now I'm here," Ivakina said. "I have enjoyed my time here; it's very nice."

Since the part of her home country where she grew up is close to the Black Sea, playing in Texas felt normal, but living in Alaska has been "quite different" and requires her to bundle up a lot more.

"It's getting cold here, so it's quite miserable, but it's been great and I've enjoyed it," Ivakina said.

She takes pride in being a leader on and off the court for the Seawolves as a playmaker and with her words, even though English isn't her first language.

"I love it, and I'm trying to do better every day, honestly," Ivakina said.

Her ability to uplift the team during matches goes beyond just her individual ability to score.

"Even if Lee has an off game, she's always contributing in some other fashion," Hooe said. "Sometimes we have to bring her out of the back row to give her a little break, but for the most part, she's our go-to gal."

Ivakina has recorded double-digit kills in 16 of 24 matches this season. Against Western Washington on Sept. 24, she tied her single-match career-high mark of 25 kills and set a new single-match career-high in points with 27.

"Lee is a phenomenal hitter," senior setter Hannah Trotter said. "She's one of those people who I know I can set her the ball no matter what and she's going to try her hardest to get the kill."

Trotter admires Ivakina's fearlessness and willingness to rise to the occasion when the team needs her the most, whether they're up, down or tied on the scoreboard.

"She's not afraid to hit the ball hard, she's going to make herself an option," Trotter said. "She's one of those hitters that every single ball, she comes to me and says, 'Set me the next ball,' and while I can't give her all of them, when you have someone like that (who) wants it that much for one, themselves and two, the team, I think it's a game changer because she has that competitive nature."

Both Trotter and Ivakina will play their final two home games as Seawolves this week when UAA takes on St. Martin's on Thursday night and Western Oregon on Saturday night.

Although she won't have any family or friends from Bulgaria in attendance when she takes the court in the main gym at the Alaska Airlines Center on Saturday night, Ivakina still expects it to be an emotional experience.

"I'm very, very excited; I'm not ready for senior night," she said. "I love the crowd, and I love playing at home; it's one of the highlights of my stay here."

Trotter also feels bittersweet about her Seawolf career coming to an end, but she's grateful and excited for the team's final two opportunities to prove themselves in front of their home fans.

"It's a fun arena to play in, it's a fun community to be in and it's just a fun environment," she said. "I think when we have things like this, it just kind of shows how special this school and this state are."

The Seawolves will close out the regular season on the road next week and are focused on winning, or at least earning another share of the Great Northwest Athletic Conference title for the second year in a row and qualifying for the Western Regional tournament.

"We're right on the cusp of potentially going to the West Regions, so if we can hopefully do our best for the next several games that we have, maybe we can see ourselves in the West," Hooe said.
