#2879. The NFL has a quarterback problem. The Ravens just became Exhibit A

The precise organizational term for the current state of the Baltimore Ravens is: screwed. It's a fate that likely awaits every other team that doesn't already have a quarterback succession plan in place, right this very moment. But Baltimore is uniquely beset by injury.

The Ravens lost all-everything quarterback Lamar Jackson on the very first play of Sunday's game against the Buffalo Bills to a thumb injury that will likely cost him the remainder of the season. Making matters infinitely worse, Baltimore subsequently lost backup Tyler Huntley to a toe injury that ended his season.

Oh, and just to add to the joy, undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano, who leaped from emergency No. 3 quarterback into the lineup on Sunday, is himself dealing with an ankle injury. It rains, it pours, and sometimes, it Biblically floods.

As for Baltimore's quarterback options now, well, Wednesday they dug through the scrapheap and pulled out Jacoby Brissett … who was just benched in Arizona in favor of Gardner Minshew.

The Ravens are in the teeth of the most vexing conundrum in the NFL right now: how to even survive, much less thrive, when your quarterback goes down. It's the most important position in team sports, by a wide margin, and consequently the most challenging one to fill. More complex offenses, more ferocious defenses, more pressure and more expectation all combine to forge a few elite quarterbacks, and shatter the rest.

You don't need any kind of advanced metrics to know what your eyes tell you: There are more teams in the NFL than there are good quarterbacks to lead them. The numbers back this up: ESPN's Total QBR stat, in this instance, is a useful benchmark. QBR doesn't simply total up yardage and touchdown passes; it incorporates a range of game situations and quarterback attributes, making it a solid starting point for discussions of elite, serviceable and unusable quarterbacks.

In the Total QBR system, a ranking of about 50 is average, and 25 is replacement-level. Players around the 50-point mark include Michael Penix Jr. (56.2), Sam Darnold (49.4) and Aaron Rodgers (49.2). So far, so good, right? At the low end of the 30 quarterbacks who have played enough to statistically qualify for the QBR leaderboard, you'll find Jameis Winston (41.4), Deshaun Watson (40.9), Daniel Jones (39.4) and Justin Herbert (32.6) — again, no real surprises there.

The Ravens had to face Josh Allen with undrafted Joe Fagnano under center after both Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley went down.

But when you look at the overall performance of all players who have taken snaps or thrown passes this season, that's when you really get a sense of how bad quarterback play can be. Fagnano (who will start Thursday while the Ravens wait for Brissett's arrival) posted a QBR of 7.4 in his emergency stint on Sunday. Tanner McKee is at 30.7 after starting, and losing, three games for Philadelphia. (He's now been benched in favor of Joe Milton, who has never started an NFL game.) And Cooper Rush posted an almost incomprehensibly bad QBR of 4.7 — again, 25 is replacement-level — in his two losing starts for Atlanta earlier this season. The Falcons might have been better off just direct-snapping to Bijan Robinson every play, and the Ravens might need to consider the same route with Derrick Henry.

We can also determine the teams at most risk of a falloff due to quarterback injury by using a less scientific but more financially-motivated metric. Yahoo Sports' Ben Fawkes has created a list of NFL starting quarterbacks' value in terms of point spreads vs. their backups — in other words, the amount of value that a single starting quarterback brings to a team's odds — and, again, the results won't surprise you.

Per Fawkes' metric, the quarterback most important to his team's point spreads is — shocker — the Bills' Josh Allen, who's responsible alone for 7.23 points against the spread. Put another way, if Allen were injured and backup Kyle Allen needed to come in, any scenario where Buffalo was favored by fewer than +7.5 would immediately flip the Bills from huge favorite to pick-em status. That's pretty damn significant! Wrap Josh Allen in bubble wrap, Buffalo!

The next most important quarterbacks: Patrick Mahomes (backup: Justin Fields), Matthew Stafford (backup: Ty Simpson), and Drake Maye (backup: Tommy DeVito). Jackson comes in at fifth, responsible for 5.89 points against the spread, but that's with Huntley as his backup. Now? The gap might be double digits.

So you can see the problem here. There already aren't enough quality quarterbacks in the NFL, and the teams that have more than one in-house — looking at you, San Francisco — aren't parting with them for anything less than obscene ransoms.

Look upon the list of backups this year in the NFL, and despair. All due respect to Trey Lance, Quinn Ewers, Sam Howell, Davis Mills and the rest of the "hey, I remember that guy" guys holding tablets on sidelines across the league, but there's just not much there there.

Which is how the Ravens have ended up turning to Brissett who, in fairness, has a QBR (48.9) only slightly worse than the aforementioned Rodgers.

The one hope that teams have is the Sam Darnold Scenario. Darnold, of course, sputtered from starter to backup, buried on San Francisco's depth chart until Minnesota resurrected his career and Seattle cemented his legacy with a Super Bowl title.

We have two extremely small sample size cases already this season: J.J. McCarthy, who seems rejuvenated with the Giants after flaming out in Minnesota, and Gardner Minshew, who checked in from 2019 to lead the Cardinals to a win over Dallas on Sunday. (McCarthy's QBR this year is 60.5 and Minshew's is 71.3, in case you were wondering.)

Beyond that, teams have to either mortgage their future with draft capital — say, overpaying for Kirk Cousins, if the Raiders were even interested in trading him — or grit their teeth and weather the storm until the offseason. When demand for quarterbacks far outstrips the supply, it's only a matter of time before someone — today, the Ravens; tomorrow, who knows? — ends up with another team's reject. That's the reality in a league now centered on, and entirely dependent on, elite quarterbacks.

#2878. Game Preview #9: Wolves at Rockets Game Thread

We at Canis Hoopus usually try to get game recaps out a full day before the games so everyone can get their jokes and thoughts going in the comments. It's what makes this community so fun for so many of us, myself included. From GIFs to shitposts to real analysis and everything in between, this is where to find it.

However, some of us were a little busy tracking some election day results. We were watching a blue wave instead of watching a wave of blue at Target Center. We were celebrating the left wing, not analyzing the wings left with Anthony Edwards out. We were… You get the point, but I have a ton of these.

Now, with the Minnesota Timberwolves heading down to Texas to face the 7-1, conference-leading Houston Rockets, it's time to flip red to blue, just as the state just did.

It's a pretty great day to wake up and rep the Wolves, and to completely destroy now-jobless Greg Abott's favorite team.

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (5-3) vs Houston Rockets (7-1)

When: Wednesday, November 4th at 7:30 PM CT

So far this season, the Houston Rockets have played fast. After being a team largely defined by defense, Houston is now winning their games the way people have been doing for decades: by simply scoring more points than their opponents.

Getting Fred VanVleet back after his season-ending injury has done a lot to organize the Rockets, but it is their ability to turn defense into offense that ultimately has turned them from a Kevin Durant shooting clinic into a well-balanced, well-oiled machine.

To their credit, the Timberwolves have been aiming to do a similar thing in the absence of Anthony Edwards. Without Ant, the Wolves have been relying on the talent and speed of LaMelo Ball.

"I like to play fast, and he's faster than no other," Ball said earlier this week.

While his driving (not the on-court kind) has gotten most of the focus in regard to his speed, Ball has been the fulcrum Minnesota needs to stay in games without their superstar wing and primary half-court shot creator.

However, it has also been Ayo Dosunmu's incredible play filling in for Edwards that really should get more attention. So much of the conversation around Ayo was about this feigned outrage over a perceived overpay, but the Illinois guard is more valuable for the Wolves than any other team. He can fill in for either Ball or Edwards when needed, and can certainly fit with anyone else in the rotation.

The Twin Turbos of Dosunmu and Bones Hyland have been the two main beneficiaries of Edwards' absence, and it's their pursuit of transition opportunities that has kept Minnesota alive.

The team is coming off a win against the Milwaukee Bucks that showed both of them off, and will now hopefully carry that momentum into Houston.

I want all of you to take a look at this clip for a moment. While on the surface it looks like Kevin Durant is once again getting irritated with what seems like a relatively innocuous question, there's a moment when Durant shifts from being annoyed to actually respecting the reporter's questions and thoughts.

That's because this is a really good goddamn question.

For years, the balance for stars has been all about involving teammates while also not shrinking under the lights. Many years ago, 538 put out an article about not just ball dominance but emotional dominance within a team structure. That piece determined that Durant was not an alpha.

It's been the same argument for much of LeBron James' career, and it is an argument that continues to be completely asinine.

The best players use their gravity to create for others as they do for themselves. How often they pull off those different things is what we use to define players as scorers or creators.

Durant has been an underrated playmaker for the entire duration of his career because of how good he is as a scorer. Just a few games ago, Jabari Smith Jr. had a 30-point game largely off of attacking closeouts that Durant put him in position to beat.

The Wolves will have to do a good job of not just defending Durant but also minimizing the ways he can enable everyone around him. That's so much easier said than done, and may not even be possible in general.

With Alperun Sengun at center, the Wolves can't move Rudy Gobert to guard Durant, so they will have to ask Jaden McDaniels and Jonathan Kuminga to guard him while not sacrificing their rebounding that has been so vital recently.

Answers, however, are easy to think of and hard to implement, so watch out for a big Durant game, even if the box score doesn't necessarily show it.

#2876. Vikings Facing Questions About Justin Jefferson’s Future: Report

MINNEAPOLIS, MINNESOTA - OCTOBER 25: Justin Jefferson #18 of the Minnesota Vikings signals for a first down after a reception against the Indianapolis Colts during the first quarter of the game at U.S. Bank Stadium on October 25, 2026 in Minneapolis, Minnesota. (Photo by Stephen Maturen/Getty Images) Getty

DETROIT, MICHIGAN - NOVEMBER 01: Justin Jefferson #18 of the Minnesota Vikings catches a touchdown against Roger McCreary #21 of the Detroit Lions during the second quarter of a game at Ford Field on November 01, 2026 in Detroit, Michigan. (Photo by Mike Mulholland/Getty Images) Getty

Although the Minnesota Vikings are 6-1 on the season, it seems like things are getting ugly in terms of the offensive side of the football. Now a Vikings analyst is beginning to wonder about Justin Jefferson's future with the organization.

It is clear that Jefferson is frustrated with the offense. He declined to speak with the media following the loss to the Detroit Lions on Sunday. With the NFL trade deadline looming next week, rumors about potential quarterback fits and offensive pieces have been a major topic of discussion. However, Jefferson's future with the organization has not been talked about a ton.

However, now it is being put under a microscope, as the Vikings offense continues to struggle and Jefferson wants to win. The Athletic's Matthew Collier and Alec Lewis went into this in a deep discussion with Garrett Greenlee, who runs the Football Analysis account on YouTube

"It feels like they're at a crossroads where, yes, of course, he (Justin Jefferson) wants to win. They're 6-1. They can seriously win. But he knows that if the offense performs like this, it's got first round out 13-10 written all over it. And then we go, what was that for? What was any of that for? They all know, right?" Collier asked.

"Yep. Yep. These are all very smart guys. They know that this train is headed in a certain direction," Collier replied.

The conversation then continued among the group. Greenlee made a startling point to Vikings fans during the conversation.

"And I'm at the point where it's like I am I genuinely would not be surprised. And I actually think that it's more than likely this is Justin Jefferson's final season in Purple if things don't turn around at all."

GettyMINNEAPOLIS, MINNESOTA – OCTOBER 25: Justin Jefferson #18 of the Minnesota Vikings signals for a first down after a reception against the Indianapolis Colts during the first quarter of the game at U.S. Bank Stadium on October 25, 2026 in Minneapolis, Minnesota. (Photo by Stephen Maturen/Getty Images)

This was certainly an interesting conversation given the recent events of Sunday's loss to the Detroit Lions. Jefferson declined to speak with the media post-game. He has been even-keeled in his frustrations with the offense on a public level when he has met with the media. However, it's clear that his production is not what has been in recent memory, and that the offense has struggled to get him targets.

Jefferson is having a career-worst season to this point. He has 28 receptions on 40 targets for 335 yards and four touchdowns. He is averaging 12 yards per catch. Jefferson did have a better day on Sunday, as he was able to record a touchdown grab in the second quarter of the game. However, it was clear that Jefferson and Kyler Murray were not on the same page.

GettyDETROIT, MICHIGAN – NOVEMBER 01: Justin Jefferson #18 of the Minnesota Vikings catches a touchdown against Roger McCreary #21 of the Detroit Lions during the second quarter of a game at Ford Field on November 01, 2026 in Detroit, Michigan. (Photo by Mike Mulholland/Getty Images)

On top of just on-field production, Jefferson's contract certainly comes into play. He is going to play on a $50 million salary cap hit in 2027 and $53 million salary cap hit in 2028. The contract was very player-friendly. It was potentially built with the idea that the wide receiver and organization could come to an agreement following the 2026 season.

It's clear that the Vikings are at a crossroads with Jefferson. He is one of the most dynamic players in the NFL when he is given the targets and opportunity to play at a high level. However, it seems like frustration is boiling over for the wide receiver, as the Vikings themselves try to figure out the quarterback room. Figuring out the room is not an easy task. Finding a franchise quarterback is even harder.

That said, Jefferson is entering the prime of his career. It would be reasonable to expect that he would want a chance to potentially play for a team that can play in more than just the first or second round of the playoffs. Hopefully, the Vikings will be able to get the job done and get to make a deep playoff push. They have the defense and the offensive weapons to get it done.

A lack of consistency has been the only thing stopping the Vikings from winning. Unfortunately, it may also drive their elite receiver out of town as well.

#2875. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2874. Chargers open 21-day practice window for LB Denzel Perryman

The Chargers opened the 21-day practice window for tight end David Njoku and linebacker Denzel Perryman, coach Jim Harbaugh announced Wednesday.

Harbaugh said on Monday that Njoku was ready to return from injured reserve.

Njoku, 30, went on injured reserve after injuring his fibula in Week 2 and missed the past five games. He caught six of eight targets for 67 yards and no touchdowns before his injury.

The Chargers have leaned on Oronde Gadsden and Charlie Kolar in Njoku's absence.

In his 10th season, Njoku has totaled 390 catches for 4,129 yards and 34 touchdowns.

Perryman went on injured reserve before the season with an elbow ligament tear, and he has missed seven games.

Perryman, 33, played only 10 games in 2025 due to a high-ankle sprain and a suspension.

#2872. Will Wade’s Tigers defeat the Privateers in season debut

It was refreshing to see Will Wade back in action Tuesday night, wearing purple and gold, roaming the sidelines, and even getting a technical foul called during the course of the game. As good as it was to see Wade back on the sideline, it was even more refreshing to see a basketball team showing some promise and ability.

The LSU Tigers defeated LSU New Orleans Privateers, winning 100-57. A few players stood out with some really good performances. Saliou Niang had 14 points, 14 rebounds, 5 assists, and 6 steals. Donavan Dent finished with a team-high 19 points, 10 assists, 2 steals, and 1 block. He also added 4 rebounds. Off the bench, Michael Ruzic, a freshman 7-foot big man, finished with 17 points, 6 rebounds, 2 assists, and 1 block.

This team played pretty well, considering that most of the players had very limited preseason action due to the eligibility issues the team was waiting to resolve. Now that this team gets to play together, the expectation will be for them to continue to grow.

It wasn't pretty the entire game, as the team still committed 12 turnovers and shot only 25% from behind the three-point line. Abdi Bashir was able to showcase his range Tuesday night, finishing 3-of-9 from three-point range and 4-of-11 from the floor, with 11 points off the bench.

Defensively, the Tigers held the Privateers to 25% shooting from the floor and only 14% from three-point range. However, they did allow 14 offensive rebounds, which will need to be addressed going forward. The Tigers also sent the Privateers to the free-throw line 38 times after committing 25 fouls. Expect this to improve as the season moves along and the team gets a better feel for playing together.

This was a really good start for the Tigers. They got the win but still have plenty of work to do. The talent is there, and so are the coaching and leadership. LSU will be back on the court Friday against Texas Southern at 7 p.m. on SECN+.

Share your thoughts on how you felt the Tigers handled the Privateers. What do you think the team needs to improve on before the next game?

#2870. Rams safety criticizes Chargers QB Justin Herbert: 'Throwing deli meat out there'

The Los Angeles Rams got the better of their crosstown counterparts on Sunday.

One of the key performers in the 24-9 victory win over the Los Angeles Chargers didn't hold back in his assessment of quarterback Justin Herbert, who threw two interceptions to push his league-leading total to 10.

"He was throwing deli meat out there," Rams safety Quentin Lake said on the St. Brown Podcast, sparking laughter from co-hosts Amon-Ra St. Brown and Equanimeous St. Brown. "He's seeing ghosts. ... I don't know what's going on, but we knew we were going to get a couple (of interceptions) – at least one."

Lake notched the first of the two interceptions in the first quarter when he jumped an out route to tight end Charlie Kolar, whom Herbert appeared to stare down. Lake ran the ball back 45 yards to set up an eventual 30-yard field goal by Brandon McManus.

Herbert again was intercepted when targeting Kolar in the third quarter, this time by Rams cornerback Trent McDuffie.

Now 0-7, the Chargers have gone 27 consecutive offensive possessions without scoring a touchdown.

Chargers coach Jim Harbaugh shrugged off the comments in his Wednesday news conference.

"As far as anyone's opinion, I don't see that," Harbaugh said. :Everybody's entitled to their opinion, that's not what I see."

He added: "Pass game, I thought he did his job exceptionally well. Two throws he'd like to have back."

Herbert, however, took accountability for the continued turnovers.

"It's bad decisions, poor mistakes by me," Herbert said Sunday in a postgame news conference. "It's something I have to clean up."

#2868. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2867. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs. Dallas Mavericks

The Spurs will look to keep their winning streak alive the Mavericks pay a visit. The two teams faced each other once already in this young season, but Dallas was without its two best players and simply couldn't keep up in the fourth quarter against a more talented opponent. Things could be different this time around.

Cooper Flagg has looked like a superstar in his second season, averaging 25-6-6 and showing the type of maturity that is not common in players his age. He's coming off a poor performance in a loss against the Kings, but his ceiling seems unlimited, and his production makes him a problem on a nightly basis. Kyrie Irving missed the game against Sacramento, as he's sitting out occasionally while he recovers from knee surgery, but the veteran point guard is averaging 21 points and five assists, and seems like the perfect partner for the developing Flagg. Both should be available on Wednesday, so it should be a competitive matchup.

While the Mavs have been solid this season, currently boasting a winning record, the Spurs are the hottest team in the league right now. After dropping their first two games, they have prevailed in their next five despite still trying to figure out their rotations, in part thanks to a fantastic stretch by Victor Wembanyama. They clearly have the talent to contend and should be considered the favorite over Dallas, but Flagg could present problems for a San Antonio team that starts a really small unit. On the other hand, the three-guard lineup could give the Mavericks fits on the perimeter while Wemby, looking for a bounceback performance after looking mortal against Utah, controls the paint en route to another victory.

Spurs Injuries: Tobias Harris – Out (calf), Jayden Quaintance – Out (knee).

Mavericks Injuries: Santi Aldama — Questionable (Knee), Tarik Biberović — Out (Heel), Max Christie — Out (Hip), Sergio de Larrea — Out (Knee), Dereck Lively II — Out (Foot).

Flagg is just in his second season, but judging by his recent performances, he seems on his way to superstardom sooner rather than later. The 19-year-old forward can do it all already, except consistently shoot from outside, and should only continue to get better. He has as good a case as anyone to be considered Wemby's biggest challenger for best player in the world in the near future, so it will be interesting to see what he can do against the most dominant defensive force in the league.

As mentioned, the Spurs don't have someone who can match up well with Flagg, at least physically, in their starting lineup, but Stephon Castle has shown he can bother bigger guys, and the big guy will be behind him to back him up.

San Antonio can win without Wembanyama playing at his highest level, but everything is easier when their best player dominates. He could do that against the Daniel Gafford – Morez Johnson Jr. duo, and he could definitely take advantage of Dusty May going small, like he did a couple of times, to terrible results, in their first matchup.

Wemby won't likely be guarded by Flagg for long, if at all, and vice versa, but whoever outplays the other could determine who emerges as the winner on Wednesday.

While the halfcourt battle will be interesting to watch, whichever team can control the transition game will have a huge advantage. The Spurs play at an average pace, but they lead the league in fastbreak points. Whenever San Antonio gets to run, the offense gets supercharged, and even lineups lacking shooting become viable.

It might be hard to get a huge advantage on the break, however, since the Mavericks allow the fourth-fewest fastbreak points in the league despite turning the ball over at a high rate. Dallas also prefers to play at a leisurely pace, so making sure they are forcing San Antonio into a halfcourt battle will likely be a priority for them.

In the first matchup between the two teams, the Mavs actually outscored the Spurs in transition, but it seemed like it was in part because, missing both Flagg and Irving, they just took every opportunity they could to run, as it was the only way they could score consistently. The Silver and Black also seemed to play down to their opponent at times, with some bad transition defense showing their lack of focus. That shouldn't happen again, or it could cost San Antonio a win.

The Spurs are more talented than the Mavericks overall, but they have at times found themselves lacking a spark outside of their main guys. The Mavericks, meanwhile, recently had great performances off the bench by Walter Clayton Jr., who dropped 20 on the Kings, and Morez Johnson Jr., who had 13 on just five shots against Sacramento. They have also had good showings from former No.1 overall pick Zaccharie Risacher and Naji Marshall this season.

One of those players will likely have to take Max Christie's starting spot, but they still have a bunch of guys who might not be dependable every night but can make an impact on any given game, as the Spurs learned while they were getting torched by the now-injured Sergio de Larrea in their first game against the Mavs.

The goal, clearly, will be to limit Irving and Flagg, but San Antonio will also have to keep an eye on the supporting cast to prevent someone from becoming an unlikely hero.

If you'd like to, you may follow along with the game on our Twitter profile (@poundingtherock) or visit our Game Thread!

#2864. Mike Vrabel addresses Patriots rumors ahead of NFL trade deadline

A pair of New England Patriots veterans found themselves in trade rumors ahead of the NFL's trade deadline.

This week, defensive tackle Christian Barmore and cornerback Carlton Davis were mentioned as potential trade candidates by Sports Illustrated's Albert Breer. According to Breer, trading either player would give New England an opportunity to add needed draft capital while getting younger.

Speaking on Wednesday, head coach Mike Vrabel responded to the report.

"I think sometimes national reports have the tendency to do that. I have not shared that information with anybody," Vrabel said. "You know, this time of year, I think that all these things kind of get drummed up. So we're focused on trying to prepare for Green Bay, make the corrections from the game. I mean, this is business as normal here. So, I don't know where a lot of these things come from. Certainly not any conversations with me."

Davis, 29, has played in four games this season while dealing with a neck injury. When healthy, he has occupied his usual starting role opposite Christian Gonzalez, with Vrabel highlighting his physicality and competitiveness.

Barmore, 27, has seen his playing time decrease slightly, playing just 50% of New England's defensive snaps this season after logging 65% last year. The defensive tackle has been productive at times, recording 3.5 sacks, his highest total since 2023.

Neither player has guaranteed money remaining on his contract beyond this season, making a potential trade a realistic possibility. However, would dealing away two defensive contributors make sense for a 5-3 Patriots team with aspirations of competing?

"I don't know. There's probably a bunch of ways we can make this team better," Vrabel responded. "You know, coach better, execute better, whether that's [adding] guys on the streets or — there's multiple ways. Again, we'd have these conversations each and every day."

Vrabel said he doesn't feel the need to discuss trade rumors with players, instead keeping conversations centered on football and the team's preparation.

The NFL trade deadline is set for Tuesday, Nov. 10, at 4 p.m. ET. With less than a week to finalize any moves, Vrabel's focus remains on the present.

"Right now, there'll be another week before the trade deadline," Vrabel said. "We'll be leaving for Munich next week. We're focused on today and how we get better and how we can run the ball better, how we can affect the quarterback. All these things that if we don't do, it's going to be tough."

New York hosts the 1-6 Bulls tonight for their second game in a week after beating them last Wednesday.

Chicago is not a team the Knicks should be too worried about, but hey, this is a matchup between sub-.500 teams, so who knows what will happen then and there.

Here's some of what we heard from the Knicks heading into today's game.

"Everything can get better… I'm not pissed about the start or upset about the start. There's a part of me… I'm a little excited that we're struggling. I want to see if we, right now, can figure it out.

"When you go through the offseason that we went through, everybody celebrates you the way that this lovely city did and the fans of the New York Knicks did, you want to be shaken and brought back to earth right away when the season starts because that grounds you.

"Right now, because we're not playing our best basketball, we're seeing some of the challenges that we need to work on. Right now we could be winning games, but it could be false reality."

On Jalen Brunson's early-season shooting struggles:

"He's taking the same shots as in the past. In my opinion, he's just missing shots. He's taking the right shots. I think all our guys are, for the most part, taking the right shots. Right now, they're not going in. And that can be for this reason, that reason, I don't know, and I'm not going to try to overanalyze it. As long as they keep taking the right shots, keep working on the shots, I think eventually they're gonna fall sooner than later."

"It happens. It happens to the best of them because everybody is definitely human and every human has their ups and their downs when it comes to shooting. That's why we have to keep embracing the little things, because they're not gonna always fall."

"You could drill it, and guys will pick up on it, but you can tell (Shamet) is a natural with it."

On whether his offseason wrist surgery has affected his shooting:

On Landry Shamet's ability to create defensive closeouts:

"He creates great closeouts, whether he's shooting or catching. He has a good knack for it.

"It's just how he operates. He just finds the right windows, whether it's benefiting him or someone else."

"He has a professionalism about it. For a person who is willing to do that — he makes our team better. Great person, great teammate. Off the court, he's a great dude and someone I enjoy being around."

On why the Knicks' defense has been ahead of their offense early in the season:

"We have great coaches. [Brendan O'Connor] and [Darren Erman], they do a great job indicating to us what we need to be doing on the court, and then guys are executing defensively and we're getting stops. That's always our goal.

On the Knicks finally increasing their pace:

"We finally played fast. We've been playing very slow, very stagnant. We finally got out, we ran."

On the champion Knicks remaining a work in progress:

"It's early in the season. I think everything's a work in progress. Last year, I feel like we probably hit our best stride in the playoffs. So we were a work in progress the whole year. That's the nature of the NBA season. You gotta keep getting better each and every day, each and every game, and be ready to go."

On the Knicks' dissatisfaction with their start to the season:

"I just think we've got to win, and I think it's still early in the season, and I don't think anybody in this locker room is satisfied with where we're at. The point is for us to continue to get better and we'll continue to do that."

On Mike Brown holding the Knicks accountable and not allowing the Nets win to disguise their problems:

"We have to be real with ourselves. And Mike's great with that, assesses things in film, calls it as it is. Our group's great with that. We all understand that by no means — we're not taking this as any potential fool's gold. The whole goal right now is to continue to get better, and we want to do that. So that's the thought process."

On learning to move without the basketball by playing three-on-three without dribbling as a child:

"I remember growing up playing full-court three-on-three with no dribble, so you just learn how to move without the ball. I'm lucky I had that implemented for me at a young age. I wish more kids and hope more kids would do that moving forward in today's game. I think it really helps you learn how to play and understand how to move and get open. So, it's kinda always been something I've tried to continue to get better at."

#2861. San Francisco Mayor Daniel Lurie debuts custom Valkyries shirt during victory parade

Mayor Daniel Lurie had his own fan fashion moment at the Golden State Valkyries' victory parade in San Francisco.

Standing next to the team's small forward, Gabby Williams, the politician sported a Valkyries-violet button-down shirt-jacket with "46" emblazoned on the back - a nod to his role as the city's 46th mayor - during the celebration along the Embarcadero on Wednesday, Nov. 4.

The shirt was made by fan apparel website Dannijo Pro and bears the inscription "Custom 1/Won For Daniel Lurie #46 'Ballhalla,'" according to a representative from the Mayor's office. Lurie purchased the shirt himself.

Oxfords from the company cost between $215 to nearly $600, although Lurie's exact violet shirt is not on the site.

Read more: The Bay Area's newest sports color? Valkyrie violet - and fans are owning it

Related: Are Valkyries fans the WNBA's best-dressed?

San Francisco Mayor Daniel Lurie sports Valkyries gear at the parade on Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026. (Matthias Gafni/S.F. Chronicle)

This Valkyries championship season has seen a variety of memorable looks among the WNBA team's fanbase. Many loyal followers treat games as an occasion to dress up beyond usual sports loyalties, with sequins, fringe and rhinestones making jerseys sparkle. Some fans have even shown up in mythological-themed costumes with horned helmets in homage to the team name.

Lurie's look on Wednesday put him in the company of city mayors across the United States who have donned their local team's gear after major victories. In June, New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani and First Lady Rama Duwaji drew widespread attention for their custom New York Knicks apparel during the team's 2026 NBA championship celebration.

Prior to Wednesday's parade, while Lurie is not generally known for wearing team threads at games, he's been a sartorial supporter of the Valkyries, declaring Sept. 25 "Violet Day" and encouraging residents to wear shades of purple on game days.

He has also spotlighted local merchandise made during Super Bowl LX, which was hosted at Levi's Stadium in Santa Clara in February, and was gifted a custom Team USA soccer jersey for San Francisco's official "Jersey Day" on July 1.

#2859. KC Concepcion, Rome Odunze headline the most-targeted pass-catchers still without a TD

Every week at this time I do some data hunting to try to find some interesting fantasy angles. Let's pop open the spreadsheets and see what's interesting as we get ready for Week 9.

The Diontae Johnson Award is a silly little thing I coined back in 2022, when Johnson somehow collected 147 targets for the Steelers but didn't score a touchdown. The drought was especially surprising because he scored 15 times in the two previous seasons. Normally we chase the opportunity in fantasy football, and we expect the scores to come one way or another. But there are always usage trends and play-calling patterns to consider, too.

So who's at the top of the DJA leaderboard through eight weeks? We're looking at the pass-catchers (receivers and tight ends) with the most targets without any touchdowns.

Concepcion, Denzel Boston and Harold Fannin Jr. all have similar target and efficiency stats in Cleveland, but the focus is on Boston from in close; Boston has eight red-zone targets (and three overall touchdowns) while Fannin has four inside-the-20 targets (and three overall touchdowns). Concepcion is stuck on three red-zone targets and no scores yet.

I'd like to bet on talent, so let's stick with Concepcion. Alas, it would be nice if the Browns had more talent in their QB room.

Talent isn't a problem in Chicago, where Caleb Williams is a star and Ben Johnson runs a smart offense. But note how the team handles its red-zone targets: Luther Burden 11 (eight catches), Colston Loveland 10 (six catches), Kalif Raymond 5 (five catches) and Rome Odunze 5 (one catch). Sure, there have been missed connections, with Odunze dropping at least one goal-line chance. But he hasn't been the first read here, either.

I don't have much to say on Schultz, and Bourne is sadly done for the year after fracturing his tibia. But what the heck is going on with the Buccaneers? Forget passing touchdowns, they've thrown for just 64 yards the last two games — including zero yards in the rain last week against Atlanta.

Although the Buccaneers are now the favorites to win the NFC South, I'd be worried about all of their passing components. Obviously the last two games are screaming outliers, but this team is good at a few specific things — playing defense (first in DVOA), run-blocking and power running. There's no reason why the Bucs can't rely on 1970s football — and surging Bucky Irving and Kenneth Gainwell — as they look to wear down opponents.

And even when the Bucs do look downfield, what does Godwin have left in his ninth season? His 6.9 yards per target is a career low and he's averaging just 30.4 yards per game. Maybe we can leave the light on for Emeka Egbuka when he heals up, but the train might be gone for Godwin.

I don't include running backs in the Diontae Johnson fun, but I suppose it is interesting to note the four backs who have the most targets without a touchdown catch:

It doesn't feel as life and death when running backs aren't turning their targets into touchdowns. We just like to see them find ancillary ways to score fantasy points, and ways to stay relevant to game script even when their teams are in hurry-up mode and/or playing from behind.

McCaffrey's had plenty of years where he ran hot with receiving touchdowns — he had seven such scores in both 2023 and 2025. But it is interesting to note that his yards per catch has dropped to 7.4 this year, and his yards per target (6.0) is the lowest it's been since his rookie year.

With Brown, we're just hoping the Cincinnati carnival doesn't leave without him as it continues the barnstorming tour. But unless the Bengals make a trade for a back (Taylor could make sense here), Brown is probably fine. Samaje Perine and Tahj Brooks are not major threats to his playing time.

Taylor is actually averaging a career-best 25.3 receiving yards per game, so his managers can't be unhappy here. He should be fine moving forward, be it with a new and contending team, or still on the Colts but aiming at a friendly schedule in the second half.

Swift leads the NFL in rushing touchdowns — he did his Jerome Bettis short-yardage imitation Monday at Seattle — so his managers are smiling. It will be interesting to see how the Bears handle the backfield workload when Kyle Monangai is healthy. When the fantasy season is complete, you'll be happy you drafted some Bears proactively.

#2857. Bo Bichette’s $42 Million Career Decision Might Have Costed Mets $115 Million 3B

Bo Bichette's decision to stay with the New York Mets has changed the team's offseason plans.

Bichette, 28, chose not to opt out of his three-year, $126 million contract this winter. He will earn $42 million in 2027, the second year of the deal.

The Mets signed Bichette in January 2026. The contract lets him opt out after each of the first two seasons. He can leave again next winter or take another $42 million in 2028.

Bichette struggled in his first season in Queens. He hit .260 with 18 home runs and 78 RBIs in 160 games and had a .702 on-base plus slugging percentage.

He also played 113 games at third base, a new position for him. The 28-year-old is going to give it another try.

The Mets are now "less inclined" to aggressively pursue Japanese slugger Teruaki Sato, The Athletic Will Sammon and Tim Britton reported Tuesday.

The Mets were among Sato's top suitors. But that might have changed.

The 27-year-old is a natural third baseman who has some outfield experience. But Bichette is the starting third baseman for New York.

He will earn $42 million in 2027. Marcus Semien and Jorge Polanco count a combined $49 million against the Mets' 2027 payroll.

Semien, the second baseman, carries $26 million of that figure. Polanco, who plays first base, carries $23 million.

ESPN's Kiley McDaniel projects Sato will sign for five years and $115 million. That averages $23 million a year.

Though there's chatter about whether Sato can continue at third base in MLB, it is highly risky for the Mets to commit that amount to another infielder.

Bichette's return leaves the Mets focused mainly on starting pitchers in free agency. They plan to actively pursue Tarik Skubal this winter.

Skubal is the top free agent on the market. He could set a record for a starting pitcher's contract.

Skubal is expected to get well above $300 million, possibly $400 million or more. The Mets and Dodgers are the leading bidders.

Pursuing him would require a significant financial commitment. Therefore, the front office might be weighing the Sato chase more carefully.

#2856. Roundtable: What would it take for YOU to trade Kyrie Irving?

The scuttlebutt over the weekend was a series of quasi-nonsense reports about the Dallas Mavericks being open to trading Kyrie Irving. On Sunday, the Mavs Moneyball Slack was on fire at the ridiculousness of such an idea, particularly with the Mavericks sitting pretty at 4-2. Then national media outlets started chiming in on the subject so I asked anyone who really had a take to answer this: what WOULD it take for you to be happy if the Mavericks traded Kyrie Irving?

Tyler: Obviously, the Mavericks shouldn't trade Kyrie just for the sake of trading Kyrie, but there are plenty of scenarios where they should listen. The two teams in particular that I'd be interested in dealing with are Houston and Detroit. Houston has a treasure trove of draft assets from Brooklyn and Phoenix, and combined with plenty of young talent on the roster, you could easily put together a deal with a couple of those attractive first-rounders and a young player. Detroit is such a perfect Kyrie team, and they have all of their draft capital plus young guys in Ebuka Okorie, Ron Holland, and others. Again, some firsts, and Okorie and Holland and I'd probably go ahead and do that.

That's not an indictment of this Mavericks team. They've gotten off to a very nice start amid a very tough schedule. However, one of the fatal flaws of the Luka Doncic era was accelerating the timeline with the Porzingis deal. The Mavericks in keeping all of these vets around, with a severe lack of assets to properly build around Cooper when he reaches his prime, is far more of a pressing issue than being the eighth seed this year. This is a five-year plan, not a one-year deal. They need to be prudent about this.

Ben: I'm ready to fully move on into the next era of Mavericks basketball. They shouldn't trade for the sake of trading, but should also be realistic about the value of a soon-to-be 35 year old small guard coming off knee surgery.

If they get an offer of a first-round pick and an expiring salary, they should take it immediately.

Michael: I fully agree we should not trade Irving. I have never been on board with trading the veterans for a bunch of picks and full-on rebuilding in that manner. There is just far too much unknown in doing so. Getting younger and cheaper could just as easily mean "getting much worse" as it could mean "getting better." NBA teams need veterans, especially Hall of Famers who are willing to play as the second option to a 19-year-old. The Mavs have that almost-impossible-to-obtain scenario, and I don't think they should let it go.

But that doesn't answer the question. The question was, "if we do" trade Irving, what does it take for me. My answer to that question is "a lot." It would take a younger player that is a direct replacement for Irving in terms of production (or a year removed from all but certainly making that jump), along with at least two FIRST-round picks in consecutive drafts no more than one year removed from this offseason. That is simply something I don't see happening.

Irving is critically important to this team, and I think he gets underestimated by a big margin when it comes to assessing his value. I understand he is currently 34 years old and coming off a major injury, but we're not in 1996. In this modern era, we have Steph Curry at almost four years older to the day than Irving. Kevin Durant is three-and-a-half years older with a major injury under his belt. LeBron James is another one… Irving is playing at a high level and clearly helping this team win. Also, Irving has a player option next year, then is likely gettable for a reasonable contract thereafter. I don't feel as though he is hurting the team or the cap in such a way that can't be solved via other moves (plus the team will drop Klay Thompson's $17M cap hit after this season by simply doing nothing).

The only way I personally would be willing to trade him is basically if it was the most obvious no-brainer trade imaginable where Dallas is getting back a young but ready player and draft capital. It can't be something that ushers in a "hope for the best" youth movement and obviously hurts the team right off the bat with hopes of the team improving over time. If the Mavs make moves, it should be with the goal of improving immediately and going forward.

Bryan: To give up Kyrie, the Mavericks would need to be either flailing around the December 15th mark (when most players in the league become available for trade) or to be bowled over with an offer worth 3 or more first-round picks in value if they are above .500 and in good seeding position. Unless Cooper Flagg's production slows down tremendously or Kyrie gets hurt, there is no reason to tear any of the core pieces of this team off of it for a paltry trade return when they could simply carry a team performing well into the play-in/offs and allow the young players to gain incredibly valuable postseason experience. Everyone is clamoring for Kyrie Irving to be shipped off to a playoff team that could use him more. After a hot early start, the Dallas Mavericks are pounding on their chests asking, "Why not us?"

Salvator: I think for this year barring an obvious overpay, I think they should keep Kyrie at least until the offseason. There is still value in making the postseason and seeing how our guys play in that environment, kinda similar to the one year CP3 played in OKC.

Kirk: I can't fathom trading him. Any contender making an offer to Dallas has to come through with picks that are from a team that projects badly, like, I'm not interested in a Detroit 2028 first-round pick (well… I guess I am a little, what a draft class). There has to be something there for me to sink my teeth into as a person looking ahead. Right now, Kyrie is too valuable and too fun to consider otherwise. But if I was forced? Two first-round picks, and ones that look good right now.

#2854. Cardinals Brissett traded to Baltimore

Wow. How things change. And quickly, right? Four days ago, QB Jacoby Brissett was warming up on the field at AT&T Stadium in Arlington, Texas, home of the Dallas Cowboys. Today, he is a member of the Baltimore Ravens.

On Wednesday, it was announced that Cardinals GM Monti Ossenfort had traded Brissett to the Ravens. Baltimore's starting QB, Lamar Jackson, and his backup, Tyler Huntley, are both injured and landed on IR. The Ravens had undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano, and UFL quarterback Austin Reed remaining.

RELATED: CARDINALS BENCH BRISSETT IN WIN OVER COWBOYS

Brissett was pulled early in the second half of the 33-30 win over the Cowboys, and his backup, veteran Gardner Minshew, was inserted. Suddenly, the offense came alive after Dallas had dominated for two quarters. Minshew orchestrated a late drive in regulation to tie the game, then led the game-winning drive in overtime that ended with a 42-yard Chad Ryland field goal for the win.

The very next day, it was announced by Cardinals head coach Mike LaFleur that Minshew would be the starting quarterback this week against the Seattle Seahawks, and his backup would become rookie Carson Beck. This meant that Brissett had gone from starter to QB3 in less than 24 hours.

Brissett was mentioned as trade bait by several media outlets and Cardinals podcasts. That became true in a very short timespan.

The details of the trade have not been released since the deal is not complete.

It is assumed that Brissett would not dress or start at home against the Jacksonville Jaguars. The game is tomorrow night on Thursday Night Football, broadcast on Prime. The deal is announced as still pending, but will be finalized this weekend. He is a seasoned veteran with a low salary and should become a very good rental for the Ravens for the remainder of the season. Currently, Baltimore is 4-4-0 and listed last in the division.

Brissett is heading back to a division he is quite familiar with, the AFC North. He played for the Cleveland Browns in 2022 and played the first 11 games while QB Deshaun Watson was on league suspension.

#2852. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2849. Why the Raptors could negate Jokic despite question marks at the five

The Toronto Raptors don't have a centre problem, and at the same time, they very much do.

When the Raptors take on the Denver Nuggets on Wednesday, they'll match up against the most dynamic offensive big man in the NBA – Nikola Jokic.

Despite Jokic's dominance, there's a realistic chance the Raptors produce his kryptonite. Denver currently ranks last in the league in offensive rebounds given up at 14.5 per game. Meanwhile, Toronto ranks first in offensive rebounds (15.3). Through sheer tenacity and effort, it could be enough to negate Jokic's wizardry.

It's been quite some time since Toronto's centre rotation was an undeniable advantage. When former head coach Nick Nurse had the luxury of deploying Marc Gasol and Serge Ibaka, it resulted in 48 minutes of reliable production.

Since then, the Raptors have failed to find consistency at the five.

This season, while early, has already set the stage for a fascinating narrative.

Jakob Poeltl looks healthier (and more mobile) than last season, and while he's shown positive signs of returning to form, the injury bug remains obsessed with the Austrian.

After missing the game against the Los Angeles Clippers, Poeltl was listed as questionable (left shoulder bursitis) on Nov. 3 and might be limited should he dress against Denver.

Meanwhile, Collin Murray-Boyles has taken a temporary step back, at least in comparison to what fans saw in the playoffs against the Cleveland Cavaliers. There was a lot of noise around Murray-Boyles being in even better shape this year. While the sophomore is shooting more threes, he's still ramping up defensively (and around the basket on offence).

Trayce Jackson-Davis has been a pleasant surprise off the bench, especially against the Orlando Magic on Oct. 28, when he finished with 10 points and 11 rebounds in 20 minutes of action.

In their last three games, the Raptors have both struggled and simultaneously managed their way against opposing bigs. Toronto hasn't lost the battle on the glass in any of the prior three contests, but centres have either had their way against the frontline or have made an obvious impact.

The Magic's Wendall Carter recorded 20 points and seven rebounds and Los Angeles Lakers' centre Walker Kessler finished with 16 points and 15 rebounds. While the Raptors used a historic 47-point fourth quarter to overcome the Los Angeles Clippers, the tide didn't really turn until Brook Lopez (12 points, six rebounds) fouled out.

With all due respect to the aforementioned centres, Jokic is another type of problem and represents an otherworldly challenge for the Raptors' energetic but undersized frontcourt.

Jokic is averaging 21.9 points, 11.4 rebounds and 6.6 assists through 21 games against the Raptors in his career. He also has four triple-doubles against Toronto.

#2848. Ravens discussion: instant reactions to the Jacoby Brissett trade

The Baltimore Ravens are acquiring Jacoby Brissett from the Arizona Cardinals, adding some much-needed quarterback depth after potential season-ending injuries to Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley. Here are Baltimore Beatdown's instant reactions to the move, and be sure to add yours in the comments section below!

I have long been a Jacoby Brissett fan. Turns out, so is Eric DeCosta! This is hardly a surprising trade considering the Ravens' injury crisis at quarterback, and Brissett is exactly the type of bridge passer I expected the team to pick up. Look, we all like Joe Fagnano, but the undrafted rookie out of UConn simply is not ready to command an NFL offense. Fans will debate if the Ravens should even be trying to win games for the rest of the season, but this franchise was never going to throw in the towel. Brissett can provide a base level of play for as long as the Ravens need it. Keeping the team's playoff hopes alive is another question entirely.

This trade seemed likely on Sunday night, but became nailed on as soon as Arizona announced Brissett would drop to QB3 on their depth chart. Although his season has been much worse than 2025 (when he had a 23/8 TD/INT ratio), he can push the ball down the field and feed the Ravens' weapons better than any of the current healthy options could. Compensation may take a while to be reported, but I suspect it won't be anything above a 5th-round pick, maybe as low as a 6th-round pick going the other way. I'm just hoping this offense is watchable. Thankfully, Brissett's debut is set to be against the Chargers. Maybe a win against Los Angeles in primetime can give the team a spark.

I'm not a fan of this. My guess is this moves means the Ravens brass is fairly confident Lamar Jackson will return this season and they want a veteran quarterback capable of keeping them within striking distance of a playoff spot. Otherwise, this truly makes no sense. In my opinion, even if Jackson is capable of coming back this year, I still think it makes no sense. This team is riddled with issues, front and center being the offensive line. While I still think it can be a good unit if they can get the desired starting five on the field, which has happened for less than a quarter of total eight games, it's still a question mark. The defense is still highly inconsistent. Their coaching staff seems to be young and learning as they go, which is fine and makes sense. But none of this adds up to a Super Bowl contending team. I am of the belief that the Ravens should be selling tradeable pieces at the deadline (Humphrey, Stanley, Andrews, Henry, Campbell, Washington, etc), locked 100% in on figuring out the Lamar Jackson extension, and building towards a massive roster overhaul to give Jesse Minter a team in his vision for 2027. Even if this trade is for as little as a 6th round pick, I don't love it. If it's for a 5th or more, I absolutely hate it and we should call DeCosta's priorities of the future into question, as well as his job.

The Ravens had to make a move to get another competent quarterback in the building and Brissett perhaps made the most sense of their options. Brissett can hopefully elevate the Ravens' offense to watchable status, but I don't expect him to move the needle very much in terms of winning games. If Lamar Jackson is able to come back at some point this season, then Brissett can hopefully keep the team afloat and in the playoff hunt, but my expectations for the rest of the season are about as low as they can get at this moment. Acquiring an established veteran like Brissett at least sends the message to players on the team that the season isn't being punted on entirely.

Jacoby Brissett provides more veteran leadership than you would have otherwise had in this QB room, and he has enough of an arm and a deep ball that he's serviceable. Not great, certainly not elite, but serviceable, which is more than what we can say about the unknowns of UDFA and practice squad crew that the Ravens had otherwise. The concern now would be what Baltimore gave up to get him (which hasn't been disclosed as of this writing) and whether they can keep him upright and healthy.

If this deal ends up being much ado about nothing as they move through a tough portion of their schedule, they can say they tried. From a roster-building standpoint, we may end up at Draft time grumbling about winning some games that didn't help them move up the board, but the front office will always prioritize trying to compete if they think things can be salvaged.

#2842. Can Arch Manning Finally Silence the Doubters?

Arch Manning entered the 2026 college football season with enormous expectations, but the Texas quarterback has experienced plenty of criticism throughout the year. After being viewed as one of the top Heisman Trophy contenders entering the season, Manning has faced questions about his consistency, decision-making and ability to lead Texas to a national championship.

Now, after putting together his best performance of the season, Manning has an opportunity to change the conversation.

The Texas quarterback has had an up-and-down season, including difficult performances in losses to Oklahoma and Florida. Those struggles led to questions about whether head coach Steve Sarkisian should consider making a quarterback change.

However, Sarkisian continued to show confidence in his starting quarterback, and Manning responded with two consecutive victories.

His latest performance against Mississippi State was especially encouraging. Manning completed 16 of 20 passes for 220 yards and three touchdowns without throwing an interception in Texas' 31-3 victory.

It was arguably his most complete performance of the season and showed what the Texas offense can look like when Manning plays confidently.

There is no denying that Manning has faced a different level of pressure because of his last name. Coming from the Manning family, expectations have followed him throughout his college career.

However, talent has never been the biggest question surrounding Manning. His arm strength, athleticism and ability to make plays outside the pocket have always made him one of the most exciting quarterbacks in college football.

The challenge now is putting everything together consistently against the toughest competition.

Texas still has important games remaining, and Manning will have opportunities to prove that his recent improvement is here to stay.

If he continues making smart decisions, protecting the football and creating explosive plays, the doubters could quickly become believers.

Manning has already shown flashes of greatness. Now he needs to show that he can deliver consistently.

The talent is there, but the next few weeks could determine whether Arch Manning finally silences the criticism and becomes the quarterback Texas expected him to be.
